I_{0}=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}].

(7.1)

The three single polarization beam observables are

\begin{split}I_{0}I^{c}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(\gamma)}],\\
I_{0}I^{s}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{y(\gamma)}],\\
I_{0}I^{\odot}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{z(\gamma)}].\end{split}

(7.2)

The three single polarization target observables are

I_{0}P_{i}=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{i(N)}].

(7.3)

The three single polarization recoil observables are

I_{0}P_{i^{\prime}}=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\sigma}^{i}].

(7.4)

More generally, the spin operator is used in place of
\hat{\sigma}^{i}
’s for higher spin particles. The 9 beam-target double polarization
observables are

\begin{split}I_{0}P^{c}_{i}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{xi(\gamma N)}],\\
I_{0}P^{s}_{i}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{yi(\gamma N)}],\\
I_{0}P^{\odot}_{i}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{zi(\gamma N)}].\end{split}

(7.5)

The 9 beam-recoil double polarization observables are

\begin{split}I_{0}P^{c}_{i^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(\gamma)}\hat{\sigma}^{i}],\\
I_{0}P^{s}_{i^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{y(\gamma)}\hat{\sigma}^{i}],\\
I_{0}P^{\odot}_{i^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{z(\gamma)}\hat{\sigma}^{i}].\end{split}

(7.6)

The 9 target-recoil double polarization observables are

I_{0}\mathcal{O}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{\alpha(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}].

(7.7)

The 27 triple polarization observables are

\begin{split}I_{0}\mathcal{O}^{c}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x\alpha(\gamma N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}],\\
I_{0}\mathcal{O}^{s}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{y\alpha(\gamma N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}],\\
I_{0}\mathcal{O}^{\odot}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{z\alpha(\gamma N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}].\end{split}

(7.8)

To measure the observables, we need to be able to relate them to differential cross sections. These are in turn related to the scattering amplitude, which is given in terms of the scattered state
\ket{\Psi}
and the state found after a measurement
\ket{\Phi}
by,

\sigma\propto|\braket{\Phi|\Psi}|^{2}.

(7.9)

While the symbol
\sigma
is usually used for total cross sections, in order to simplify the
notation we will take it to refer to the differential cross section (Remember that the SDME’s are not only functions of the spin projections, but also of the scattering angle
\theta
).
If the scattered state is instead a mixed state, it is not represented by the state vector
\ket{\Psi}
but rather by a density matrix
\hat{\rho}(\Psi)
. It can be shown from the definition of the
density matrix in eqn. (
4.5
) that the probability of finding a member of the statistical ensemble in state
\Phi
after
a measurement is given by

\sigma\propto\braket{\Phi|\hat{\rho}(\Psi)|\Phi}.

(7.10)

We will soon show that the polarization observables are related to ratios of sums of cross sections, which means that the proportionality constant that turns eqns. (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) into an equality is irrelevant. Therefore, for the rest of this thesis we will instead use

\sigma\propto\braket{\Phi|\hat{\rho}(\Psi)|\Phi}\Rightarrow\sigma=\braket{\Phi|\hat{\rho}(\Psi)|\Phi}.

(7.11)

Since we can now relate matrix elements of density matrices to cross sections, we can relate the observables defined in eqns.
(
7.1
) to (
7.8
) to cross sections. The expression for the spin density matrix of the recoil baryon shown in eqn. (
6.15
) depends on the state of the beam and target through the quantities
\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)}
and

\vec{\Lambda}^{(N)}
. We will therefore write it as

\hat{\rho}(B)\equiv\hat{\rho}(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\vec{\Lambda}^{(N)}).

(7.12)

Each of the polarization vectors in the previous equation have three components, so the spin density matrix of the recoil baryon requires six quantities to specify the spin state of the beam and target that gave rise to it. However, in the rest of this section we will assume that the experiments will be set up such that the beam and target are either polarized along one of
the three Cartesian coordinate axes (
x
,
y
or
z
), or unpolarized. Therefore, it will depend on two quantities: the two degrees of polarization of the beam and target along an axis. From eqn. (
7.11
) we see that the cross section for measuring the recoil nucleon with spin projection
\lambda_{i}
along the

i
-axis from a reaction in which the photon beam and target nucleon are fully polarized along the
j
- and
k
-axis respectively, is given by

\sigma_{i;jk}(\pm;\pm,\pm)=\braket{\lambda_{i}=\pm\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{j(\gamma)}=\pm 1,\Lambda^{k(N)}=\pm 1)|\lambda_{i}=\pm\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}.

(7.13)

This gives us eight possible polarized cross sections.

For example, the cross section for the recoil nucleon to be measured with spin projection
-1/2
along the
y
-axis when the beam is polarized with spin projection
-1
along the

z
-axis (circularly polarized beam) and the
target is polarized with spin projection
1/2
along the
x
-axis is

\sigma_{y;zx}(-;-,+)=\braket{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=+1)|\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}.

(7.14)

If the beam or target are unpolarized,
\Lambda^{i}=0
for the corresponding hadron. By using this identity that can be shown from eqn. (
6.15
),

\hat{\rho}(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\Lambda^{z(N)}=0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\hat{\rho}(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\Lambda^{z(N)}=1)+\hat{\rho}(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\Lambda^{z(N)}=-1)\Big),

(7.15)

we can show that, if in the expression shown in eqn. (
7.14
) the target baryon was instead unpolarized, the cross section would be

\begin{split}\sigma_{y;zz}(-;-,0)&=\braket{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{z(N)}=0)|\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\\
&=\bra{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\frac{1}{2}\Big(\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{z(N)}=1)\\
&\hphantom{=}\qquad+\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{z(N)}=-1)\Big)\ket{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\braket{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{z(N)}=1)|\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\\
&\qquad+\braket{\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{z(N)}=-1)|\lambda_{y}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\Big]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\big[\sigma_{y;zz}(-;-,+)+\sigma_{y;zz}(-;-,-)\big].\end{split}

(7.16)

The last line of the previous expression shows that a cross section with an unpolarized beam or target is equal to the average of the polarized cross sections with the two possible orthogonal spin states in some basis. Similarly, if the polarization of the recoil nucleon is not measured, its cross section is equal
to the sum of the cross sections with all possible polarizations in some basis. For example, if the photon has spin projection
-1
along the
z
-axis, the target has spin projection
+1/2
along the
x
-axis and the recoil polarization is not measured, the cross section is

\begin{split}\sigma_{z;zx}(0;-,+)&=\sigma_{z;zx}(+;-,+)+\sigma_{z;zx}(-;-,+)\\
&=\braket{\lambda_{z}=\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)|\lambda_{z}=\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\braket{\lambda_{z}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)|\lambda_{z}=-\scalebox{.8}{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\\
&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)].\end{split}

(7.17)

Therefore, the trace of the spin density matrix is taken when the recoiling particle’s polarization is not measured.

We can now write the observables in terms of polarized differential cross sections. For the observable
I_{0}
, we get

\begin{split}I_{0}&\equiv\Tr[\rho^{0}]\\
&=\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{z(N)}=0)]\\
&=\sigma_{z;zz}(0;0,0).\end{split}

(7.18)

In other words, this observable is simply the unpolarized cross section (up to a spin-independent constant). Written in terms of polarized cross sections,

\begin{split}I_{0}&=\frac{1}{4}[\sigma_{z;zz}(+;+,+)+\sigma_{z;zz}(+;+,-)+\sigma_{z;zz}(-;+,+)+\sigma_{z;zz}(-;+,-)\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sigma_{z;zz}(+;-,+)+\sigma_{z;zz}(+;-,-)+\sigma_{z;zz}(-;-,+)+\sigma_{z;zz}(-;-,-)]\\
&=\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{0},\end{split}

(7.19)

where we have defined the quantity inside the brackets on the first line as
\sigma_{0}
(the factor of
\frac{1}{4}
comes from averaging over the initial polarizations for the photon and the target nucleon).

To find expressions for the beam or target polarization observables, we need to be able to find the 16 matrices
\hat{\rho}^{i}
from
\hat{\rho}(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\vec{\Lambda}^{(N)})
. To find
\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}
, for example, we use eqn. (
6.15
) to find,

\hat{\rho}^{x}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{x(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)-\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{x(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)}=-1)\Big).

(7.20)

Therefore, the observable
P^{x}
is
given by

\begin{split}P^{x}&\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}\\
&=\frac{\frac{1}{2}\big\{\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)]-\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)]}=-1)]\big\}}{\frac{1}{2}\big\{\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)]+\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{x(N)}=-1)]\big\}}\\
&=\frac{\sigma_{z;zx}(0;0,+)-\sigma_{z;zx}(0;0,-)}{\sigma_{z;zx}(0;0,+)+\sigma_{z;zx}(0;0,-)}.\end{split}

(7.21)

This shows that the target polarization observables are simply the asymmetry in the polarized cross sections along some axis, normalized over the unpolarized cross section, at each value of the scattering angle. Therefore, in order to measure this observable in the lab,
the experimenter would have to run the experiment with the target polarized along the positive
x
-axis and measure the scattering
cross section without measuring the spin of the recoil nucleon, and then repeat the measurement with the experiment set with the target polarized along
the negative
x
-axis. The difference between these two quantities divided by their sum gives you the observable. If written in terms of all the polarized
cross sections, we get,

\begin{split}P^{x}&=\big[\sigma_{z;zx}(+;+,+)+\sigma_{z;zx}(+;-,+)+\sigma_{z;zx}(-;+,+)+\sigma_{z;zx}(-;-,+)\\
&\hphantom{=}-\sigma_{z;zx}(+;+,-)-\sigma_{z;zx}(+;-,-)-\sigma_{z;zx}(-;+,-)-\sigma_{z;zx}(-;-,-)\big]\Bigg/\sigma_{0}.\end{split}

(7.22)

In general, every single observable will be a linear combination of all of the eight polarized cross sections normalized over the unpolarized cross section. For each observable, the relative minus signs among the eight terms will be different.

To find the expressions for the recoil observables, we need to use the identity

\Tr[\hat{A}\hat{\sigma}^{z}]=\braket{+,z|\hat{A}|+,z}-\braket{-,z|\hat{A}|-,z},

(7.23)

where
\hat{A}
is any
2\times 2
matrix. Therefore, the observable
P_{z^{\prime}}
can be expressed as

\begin{split}P_{z^{\prime}}&\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\sigma}^{z}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}\\
&=\frac{\braket{+,z|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{z(N)}=0)|+,z}-\braket{-,z|\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{z(N)}=0)|-,z}}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{z(\gamma)}=0,\Lambda^{z(N)}=0]}\\
&=\frac{\sigma_{z;zz}(+;0,0)-\sigma_{z;zz}(-;0,0)}{\sigma_{z;zz}(+;0,0)+\sigma_{z;zz}(-;0,0)}.\end{split}

(7.24)

Once again, we get an asymmetry in the cross sections of measuring the recoil nucleon along some axis, normalized over the
unpolarized cross section.

In order to find the expression for
P_{x^{\prime}}
or
P_{y^{\prime}}
, we need these relations, which are correct up to an irrelevant phase factor,

\begin{split}&\hat{\sigma}^{x}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\sigma}^{z}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big),\quad\hat{\sigma}^{y}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{x}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\sigma}^{z}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{x}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big),\\
&\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\ket{\pm,z}=\ket{\pm,x},\quad\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{x}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\ket{\pm,z}=\ket{\pm,x},\end{split}

(7.25)

where
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{i}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\pm\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)
is the rotation matrix around the
i
-axis by
\pm\pi/2

radians. The expression for the observable
P_{x}
is therefore

\displaystyle P_{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\sigma}^{x}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\sigma}^{z}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\sigma}^{z}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}

(7.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{\braket{+,z|\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)|+,z}-\braket{-,z|\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)\hat{\rho}^{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{y}\big(\scalebox{1}{$\frac{\pi}{2}$}\big)|-,z)}}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\braket{+,x|\hat{\rho}^{0}|+,x}-\braket{-,x|\hat{\rho}^{0}|-,x)}}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{x;zz}(+;0,0)-\sigma_{x;zz}(-;0,0)}{\sigma_{x;zz}(+;0,0)+\sigma_{x;zz}(-;0,0)},

where in the third line of the previous equation we used the cyclic property of the trace.
Once again, we get an asymmetry, this time for measuring the recoil baryon’s spin along the
x
-axis.

For a double polarization observable, we find (using the beam-target observable
P^{s}_{x}
as
an example),

\displaystyle P^{s}_{x}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{yx}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]},

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\Big(\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{y(\gamma)}=1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)]-\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{y(\gamma)}=1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=-1)]\Big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{y(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=1)]-\Tr[\hat{\rho}(\Lambda^{y(\gamma)}=-1,\Lambda^{x(N)}=-1)]\Big)\bigg\}\Bigg/\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]

(7.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,+)-\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,-)]-\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,+)-\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,-)]\bigg\}}{\frac{1}{4}[\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,+)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,-)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,+)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,-)]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,+)-\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,-)-\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,+)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,-)}{\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,+)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;+,-)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,+)+\sigma_{z;yx}(0;-,-)}.

Note from the third line of the last equation that a double polarization observable is an asymmetry of an asymmetry, i.e., an
asymmetry in the target is measured for each of the two values of the beam polarization, and then the asymmetry between these two values is
taken.

Recall that eqn. (
6
) shows that the 64 SDME’s are equal to bilinear combinations of the helicity amplitudes in eqn. (
6.5
). The observables can therefore also be expressed in terms of said amplitudes. As such, measurements of observables can be used to extract the helicity amplitudes of the process at different kinematic points. As previously mentioned, the behaviour of these amplitudes as a function of the kinematic variables is needed to carry out the PWA’s, which could establish the presence of the resonances contributing to the reactions. Eqn. (
6.5
) shows there are 8 complex amplitudes in double pion photoproduction, for a total 16 parameters. Since a state vector describes a quantum state up to an overall phase factor, it should come as no surprise that 15 parameters can be extracted from the measurement of the observables.

Substituting the expressions in eqn. (
6
) into the definitions of the observables in eqns. (
7.1
)-(
7.8
) allows us to write the observables in terms of the helicity amplitudes. The unpolarized cross section is

\displaystyle I_{0}\equiv\Tr[\rho^{0}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Big(|M_{+,++}|^{2}+|M_{+,-+}|^{2}+|M_{+,+-}|^{2}+|M_{+,--}|^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+|M_{-,++}|^{2}+|M_{-,-+}|^{2}+|M_{-,+-}|^{2}+|M_{-,--}|^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle=|M_{+,++}|^{2}+|M_{+,-+}|^{2}+|M_{+,+-}|^{2}+|M_{+,--}|^{2}

(7.28)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+|M_{-,++}|^{2}+|M_{-,-+}|^{2}+|M_{-,+-}|^{2}+|M_{-,--}|^{2},

which is what is expected for the unpolarized cross section. To show a few more examples, the single polarization observables for the
beam are given by

\begin{split}I^{\odot}\equiv\frac{\Tr[\rho^{z(\gamma)}]}{\Tr[\rho^{0}]}&=\bigg(\Big[|M_{+,++}|^{2}+|M_{+,-+}|^{2}+|M_{-,++}|^{2}+|M_{-,-+}|^{2}\Big]\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Big[|M_{+,+-}|^{2}+|M_{+,--}|^{2}+|M_{-,+-}|^{2}+|M_{-,--}|^{2}\Big]\bigg)\Bigg/\sigma_{0},\end{split}

(7.29)

\begin{split}I^{c}\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(\gamma)}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]},&=2\Re\Big[M_{+;++}M^{*}_{+;+-}+M_{+;-+}M^{*}_{+;--}\\
&\hphantom{=}+M_{-;++}M^{*}_{-;+-}+M_{-;-+}M^{*}_{-;--}\Big]\Bigg/\sigma_{0},\end{split}

(7.30)

\begin{split}I^{s}\equiv\frac{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{y(\gamma)}]}{\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{0}]},&=2\Im\Big[M_{+;++}M^{*}_{+;+-}+M_{+;-+}M^{*}_{+;--}\\
&\hphantom{=}+M_{-;++}M^{*}_{-;+-}+M_{-;-+}M^{*}_{-;--}\Big]\Bigg/\sigma_{0}.\end{split}

(7.31)

Note that all observables are therefore the ratio of a bilinear sum of the transition amplitudes and the unpolarized cross section.

Eqn. (
6.15
) shows the spin density matrix of the nucleon
\hat{\rho}(B)
is a function of 16 expansion matrices. But since the observables are functions of the SDME’s of these matrices, it can alternatively be expressed in terms of the observables. To show this, we will need these identities,

\Tr[\hat{\sigma}^{i}]=0,\quad\Tr[\hat{\sigma}^{i}\hat{\sigma}^{j}]=\delta_{ij}.

(7.32)

Since any
2\times 2
matrix can be expressed as a linear combination of the
2\times 2
identity and three Pauli matrices, we can do so for the 16 matrices in the expansion in eqn. (
6.15
). As an example, we will write the matrix
\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}
as

\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}=A+\sum_{i}B_{xj}\hat{\sigma}^{j},

(7.33)

where
A
and the
B_{xj}
’s are as-of-yet-unknown expansion coefficients (the first term has an implied factor of a
2\times 2
identity matrix). To find
A
, we take the trace on both sides of the equation,

\begin{split}&I_{0}\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}]=2I_{0}A=P_{x},\\
&\rightarrow A=\frac{1}{2}\frac{P_{x}}{I_{0}}\end{split}

(7.34)

(The factor of
2
comes from taking the trace of the identity matrix). To find the
B_{xj}
’s, we multiply both sides by a Pauli matrix and take the trace,

\begin{split}&I_{0}\Tr[\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{i}]=2I_{0}B_{xi}=\mathcal{O}_{xi^{\prime}},\\
&\rightarrow B_{xi}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathcal{O}_{xi^{\prime}}}{I_{0}}.\end{split}

(7.35)

Therefore,

\hat{\rho}^{x(N)}=P_{x}+\sum_{i}\mathcal{O}_{xi^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i}.

(7.36)

Applying this derivation to all 16 matrices, we find

\begin{split}\hat{\rho}^{0}&=\frac{1}{2}\Big(I_{0}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}P_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{i(N)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(P_{i}+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{i\beta^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{x(\gamma)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(I^{c}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}P^{c}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{y(\gamma)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(I^{s}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}P^{s}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{z(\gamma)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(I^{\odot}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}P^{\odot}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{xi(\gamma N)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(P^{c}_{i}+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{c}_{i\beta^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{yi(\gamma N)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(P^{s}_{i}+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{s}_{i\beta^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\Big),\\
\hat{\rho}^{zi(\gamma N)}&=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Big(P^{\odot}_{i}+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{\odot}_{i\beta^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\Big).\\
\end{split}

(7.37)

By substituting these expressions into eqn. (
6.15
) we get

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(B)I\big(\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)},\vec{\Lambda}^{(N)}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}I_{0}\Bigg\{\Big(1+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}P_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}P_{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}\Lambda^{\alpha(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\delta_{\odot}\Big(I^{\odot}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}P^{\odot}_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}P^{\odot}_{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}\Lambda^{\alpha(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{\odot}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}\Big)

(7.38)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\delta_{l}\bigg[\sin 2\beta\Big(I^{s}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}P^{s}_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}P^{s}_{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}\Lambda^{\alpha(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{s}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\cos 2\beta\Big(I^{c}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}P^{c}_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}}\hat{\sigma}^{i^{\prime}}P^{c}_{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}\Lambda^{\alpha(N)}\hat{\sigma}^{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{c}_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg]\Bigg\},

where in this expression the density matrix
\hat{\rho}(B)
is normalized so that its trace is equal to
1
,

I
is the reaction rate when the spin of the recoil nucleon is not measured and is
a function of the initial polarizations
\vec{\Lambda}^{(\gamma)}
and
\vec{\Lambda}^{(N)}
.

As established previously, the spin density matrix
\hat{\rho}(B)
, can also be expressed in terms of the statistical tensors instead of the SDME’s of the 16 matrices in eqn. (
6.15
). By combining this equation with eqn. (
5.5
), we get

\hat{\rho}(B)=\sum_{L=0}^{1}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}(t_{LM}^{0}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}t_{LM}^{i(\gamma)}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}t_{LM}^{i(N)}+\sum_{i,j}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}t_{LM}^{ij(\gamma N)})\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM},

(7.39)

where, for example,

t_{LM}^{i(\gamma)}=\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=-1/2}^{1/2}(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}-m}\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{mm^{\prime}}C^{LM}_{\frac{1}{2}m^{\prime}\frac{1}{2}-m}

(7.40)

(The sum goes over
-1/2
and
1/2
because the recoil nucleon
B
is spin-1/2). The inverse relation of eqn. (
7.40
) is

\begin{split}\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{mm^{\prime}}&=\sum_{L=0}^{1}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}t_{LM}^{i(\gamma)}[\hat{T}_{LM}]_{m^{\prime}m},\\
&=\sum_{L=0}^{1}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}-m^{\prime}}t_{LM}^{i(\gamma)}C^{LM}_{\frac{1}{2}m^{\prime};\frac{1}{2}-m}.\end{split}

(7.41)

By comparing eqns. (
7.39
) and (
5.5
), we see that just as the spin density matrix can be expanded into a linear combination
of 16 other matrices, the statistical tensors can also be expanded into a linear combination of 16 other tensors,

t_{LM}=t_{LM}^{0}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}t_{LM}^{i(\gamma)}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}t_{LM}^{i(N)}+\sum_{ij}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}t_{LM}^{ij(\gamma N)}.

(7.42)

To find the expression relating the polarization observables to these statistical tensors, all that is needed is to combine equation
(
7.41
) with eqns. (
7.1
) through (
7.8
) and use the trace property of the polarization
operators,

\Tr[\hat{T}_{LM}(S)]=\sqrt{2S+1}\delta_{L0}\delta_{M0}.

(7.43)

The unpolarized observable is

I_{0}=\sqrt{2}t^{0}_{00}.

(7.44)

The three beam single polarization observables are given by

\begin{split}I_{0}I^{c}&=\sqrt{2}t^{x(\gamma)}_{00},\\
I_{0}I^{s}&=\sqrt{2}t^{y(\gamma)}_{00},\\
I_{0}I^{\odot}&=\sqrt{2}t^{z(\gamma)}_{00}.\end{split}

(7.45)

All of the observables that do not involve the recoil baryon will have the same form as eqns. (
7.44
) and (
7.45
), with the only difference among them being the superscript of
t_{00}
(
x(N)
,
z(\gamma)
,
y(\gamma N)
, etc).
To find the expressions for the recoil polarizations, we need to express the Pauli matrices in terms of the spherical
components of the recoil baryon’s spin operator,

\begin{split}\hat{\sigma}_{z}=&2\hat{S}_{0}=\sqrt{2}\hat{T}_{10},\\
\hat{\sigma}_{x}=&2(\hat{S}_{-1}-\hat{S}_{+1})=\sqrt{2}(\hat{T}_{1-1}-\hat{T}_{11}),\\
\hat{\sigma}_{y}=&i2(\hat{S}_{-1}+\hat{S}_{+1})=i\sqrt{2}(\hat{T}_{1-1}+\hat{T}_{11}),\end{split}

(7.46)

so that we can we can exploit the property

\Tr[\hat{T}_{L_{1}M_{1}}\hat{T}_{L_{2}M_{2}}]=(-1)^{M_{1}}\delta_{L_{1}L_{2}}\delta_{M_{1}-M_{2}}.

(7.47)

Using eqns. (
7.46
), (
7.47
) and (
5.10
) along with the equations for the observables that involve the recoil baryon from eqns. (
7.1
) to (
7.8
), we get for example

\begin{split}I_{0}P_{z^{\prime}}=&\sqrt{2}t^{0}_{10},\\
I_{0}P_{x^{\prime}}=&\sqrt{2}(t^{0}_{11}-t^{0}_{1-1})=2\sqrt{2}\Re[t^{0}_{11}],\\
I_{0}P_{y^{\prime}}=&-i\sqrt{2}(t^{0}_{11}+t^{0}_{1-1})=2\sqrt{2}\Im[t^{0}_{11}],\end{split}

(7.48)

where in the last two lines the hermiticity property of the tensors shown in eqn. (
5.10
) was used. All observables that involve the recoil baryon will have this form. The ones involving the
z
-projection of the recoil baryon will be proportional to a
t_{10}
, while those involving the
x
- and
y
-projections of the recoil baryon are proportional to the real and imaginary parts of a
t_{11}
, respectively. The only difference among them will be the superscript of the tensor. For example, the observable

\begin{split}I_{0}\mathcal{O}_{zx^{\prime}}&=2\sqrt{2}\Re[t^{z(N)}_{11}]\end{split}

(7.49)

has a
z(N)
superscript because it involves the
z
-projection of the target, and is proportional to the real part of the tensor because it involves the
x
-projection of the recoil baryon.

For comparison, the same observables in eqns. (
7.45
) and (
7.48
) in terms of the SDME’s are,

\begin{split}I_{0}I^{c}&=\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}+\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}},\\
I_{0}I^{s}&=\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}},\\
I_{0}I^{\odot}&=\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}+\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}},\\
\end{split}

(7.50)

and

\begin{split}I_{0}P_{z^{\prime}}&=\rho^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}-\rho^{0}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}},\\
I_{0}P_{x^{\prime}}&=\rho^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}+\rho^{0}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}=2\Re\Big[\rho^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}\Big],\\
I_{0}P_{y^{\prime}}&=i\bigg(\rho^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}-\rho^{0}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)=-2\Im\Big[\rho^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}\Big].\end{split}

(7.51)

While the SDME’s and the statistical tensors contain the same information, it can be seen from this comparison that expressions for the observables in terms of the tensors are simpler than the ones in terms of the SDME’s. This, in addition to the fact that the tensors transform under irreducible representations of the rotation group (the well known Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-matrices) and the fact that it allows the use of the Wigner-Eckart theorem shown in eqn. (
5.12
) in theoretic calculations, makes them a more natural choice in describing the observables. Nevertheless, since the use of SDME’s is widespread in the literature, we will express all results in this thesis in terms of both the SDME’s and the statistical tensors.

Chapter 8
Angular Distributions in
\gamma N\rightarrow M^{*}B\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
,
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B^{*}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
, and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B^{*}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B

The previous section dealt with polarization observables in photoproduction reactions without considering the reaction mechanism. Since it is well known that many of the contributions to this reaction are quasi-two-body states, we will consider them in the rest of this thesis. As mentioned in section
3
, the three possible channels are
\gamma N\rightarrow M^{*}B\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
,
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B^{*}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
, and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B^{*}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
, where
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
M^{*}
are mesons while
B
and
B^{*}
are baryons. The goal of this section is to find an expressions that relates the decay distribution and the polarization observables of the photoproduction reaction to the SDME’s or statistical tensors of the decaying hadron (
M^{\prime}
or
B^{\prime}
). For now, we will make the
assumption that only one such quasi-two-body state contributes to the reaction. The examples we’ll consider are the reactions
\gamma N\rightarrow VN\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}N
, where
V
is a vector meson,
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}N
, where
B_{3/2}
is a spin-3/2 baryon, and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}N
. This procedure is also laid out in a somewhat different mathematical language in refs.
[
93
,
92
]
.

The
VB
system has a massive spin-1 hadron (3 orthogonal spin states) and a spin-1/2 hadron (2 orthogonal states). Therefore, the state vector in the ensemble can be represented by a
6\times 1
column matrix,

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi^{i}(VB)}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{i}_{1\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi^{i}_{1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi^{i}_{0\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi^{i}_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi^{i}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi^{i}_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\end{pmatrix},

(8.1)

where
i
labels a members of the statistical ensemble in the mixed state. Since the initial state shown in eqn. (
6.3
) is represented by a
4\times 1
column matrix, the transition matrix will be
6\times 4
,

\displaystyle\hat{M}(\theta;\gamma N\rightarrow VB)=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{0\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\end{pmatrix}.

(8.2)

From the definition of the density matrix in eqn. (
4.5
), the density matrix of the
VB
system is
6\times 6
.

Just like we have done in eqn. (
6.8
), we can obtain the density matrix of the
VB
system from that of the initial state by,

\hat{\rho}(VB)=\hat{M}\hat{\rho}(\gamma N)\hat{M}^{{\dagger}},

(8.3)

or, in terms of the individual matrix elements,

\rho(VB)_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B},\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\rho_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}}(\gamma N)M^{*}_{\lambda_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}^{\prime};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}},

(8.4)

where the
\lambda_{\gamma}
,
\lambda_{N}
,
\lambda_{V}
, and
\lambda_{B}
are the helicities of the beam, target, vector meson, and recoil nucleon respectively. Next, the state will transition once again, this time from the
VB
state to the
M_{1}M_{2}B
state through the decay of the vector meson into
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. The transition matrix for the two body decay of an arbitrary particle at rest of spin
S
,
A\rightarrow BC
, is proportional to a Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-matrix,

M_{\lambda_{B}\lambda_{C};\lambda_{A}}\propto D^{S*}_{\lambda_{A}(\lambda_{B}-\lambda_{C})}.

(8.5)

Since the
B
subsystem is a spectator during the decay of the vector meson, the transition matrix is therefore the tensor product of two transition matrices, one acting on the
V

subsystem and the other on the
B
subsystem,

\hat{M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};VB\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B)=\hat{D}^{1\dagger}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\otimes\hat{I},

(8.6)

where
\hat{I}
is the identity matrix, and
\hat{D}^{1\dagger}
is the
1\times 3
matrix

\begin{split}\hat{D}^{1\dagger}&=c\begin{pmatrix}D^{1*}_{10}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{1*}_{00}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{1*}_{-10}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\end{pmatrix}\\
&=c\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{e^{-i\phi^{*}}\sin(\theta^{*})}{\sqrt{2}}&\cos(\theta^{*})&\frac{e^{i\phi^{*}}\sin(\theta^{*})}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(8.7)

The
{D^{1*}_{\lambda_{V}0}}
are complex conjugates of the Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-functions, which are functions of the decay angles
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}

defined in section
3
, and
c
is a proportionality constant that won’t be relevant in these discussions, and so will be dropped for the rest of this thesis. The state of the
B
subsystem is
being multiplied by the identity matrix because the final nucleon does not transition into new particles
(it does not decay). We can use the transition matrix shown in eqn. (
8.6
) to find the spin density matrix of the
M_{1}M_{2}B
system,

\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2}B)=(\hat{D}^{1\dagger}\otimes\hat{I})\hat{\rho}(VB)(\hat{D}^{1}\otimes\hat{I}),

(8.8)

or, using index notation,

\rho_{\lambda_{B}\lambda_{B}^{\prime}}(M_{1}M_{2}B)=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}^{\prime}}(BV)D^{1}_{\lambda_{V}^{\prime}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

(8.9)

We showed in eqn. (
7.11
) that the trace of the spin density matrix is proportional to the cross section. We therefore take the trace of the previous equation,

\begin{split}\Tr[\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2}B)]=&\sum_{\lambda_{B}}\rho_{\lambda_{B},\lambda_{B}}(M_{1}M_{2}B)\\
=&\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big[\sum_{\lambda_{B}}\rho_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}(VB)\Big]D^{1}_{\lambda_{V}^{\prime}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).\end{split}

(8.10)

The expression in square brackets in the second line of the previous equation is called a partial trace over the spin density matrix,

\begin{split}\Big[\sum_{\lambda_{B}}\rho(VB)_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}\Big]\equiv&\rho_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V),\\
\Tr_{B}[\hat{\rho}(VB)]\equiv&\hat{\rho}(V).\end{split}

(8.11)

The subscript
B
on the second line of the previous equation means that we are taking the trace only over the indices of the recoil baryon
B
. In terms of experiments, taking the trace over recoil baryon indices means that its spin is not being measured. This removes the spin information of the recoil baryon that the density matrix contains. What we end up with is therefore considered the spin density matrix of the vector meson
V
subsystem. We can then consider only the process of the decay of the
V
into
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. Equation (
8.10
) therefore simplifies to

\begin{split}\Tr[\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2})]=&\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)D^{1}_{\lambda_{V}^{\prime}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
\equiv&W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V),\end{split}

(8.12)

(note that since
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are spinless, its density matrix
\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2})
is just a real number. While in general a trace must be taken, in this example there is technically no need to take a trace). Since the trace is proportional to the cross section of a decay process, and since its angular dependence is known, we can also call it the angular distribution of the decay products
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, which we designate as
W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)
. Using the explicit forms of the Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-functions, the decay rate is

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\rho_{00}(V)\cos^{2}\theta^{*}+\frac{1}{2}(\rho_{11}(V)+\rho_{-1-1}(V))\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Re[\rho_{10}(V)-\rho_{0-1}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Im[\rho_{10}(V)-\rho_{0-1}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Re[\rho_{1-1}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}+\Im[\rho_{1-1}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(8.13)

where we have used the hermiticity condition of the density matrix in eqn. (
4.8
) to express
W
only in terms of the independent elements. Note that, since the diagonal elements are purely real, the distribution is real, as expected.

In the case of the
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B^{*}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B_{3/2}
, we can also find the distribution of the decay products using the same procedure. This time the
\gamma N
systems in the ensemble transition into a
M_{1}B^{\prime}
state with spin state vector

\ket{\Psi^{i}(M_{1}B_{3/2})}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{i}_{\frac{3}{2}}(M_{1}B_{3/2})\\
\psi^{i}_{\frac{1}{2}}(M_{1}B_{3/2})\\
\psi^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}(M_{1}B_{3/2})\\
\psi^{i}_{-\frac{3}{2}}(M_{1}B_{3/2})\end{pmatrix}.

(8.14)

Having four possible spin states, the spin transition matrix is therefore
4\times 4
,

\hat{M}(\theta;\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2})=\begin{pmatrix}M_{\frac{3}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{3}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{3}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{3}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\\
M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{3}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{3}{2};1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{3}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)&M_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{3}{2};\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta)\end{pmatrix}.

(8.15)

Just as in the example of a decaying vector meson, we can write the spin density matrix of the
M_{1}M_{2}B
system in terms of the spin density matrix of the
M_{1}B_{3/2}
system,

\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2}B)=\hat{D}^{3/2\dagger}\hat{\rho}(M_{1}B_{3/2})\hat{D}^{3/2}.

(8.16)

Since
M_{1}
is spinless and only a spectator in the decay of
B_{3/2}
, we can instead write it as

\hat{\rho}(M_{2}B)=\hat{D}^{3/2\dagger}\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2})\hat{D}^{3/2},

(8.17)

where we call
\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2})
the spin density matrix of the baryon subsystem
B_{3/2}
. Using index notation, it can be expressed as

\rho_{\lambda_{B}\lambda_{B}^{\prime}}(M_{2}B)=\sum_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}D^{3/2*}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

(8.18)

where the transition matrix for the decay is now

\hat{\cal{D}}^{3/2\dagger}=c\begin{pmatrix}D^{3/2*}_{\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&D^{3/2*}_{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\end{pmatrix},

(8.19)

where
c
is a proportionality constant. This matrix in terms of the explicit expressions for the Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-functions
is too large to be displayed in this article. Just as in the example of the decay of the vector meson
V
, taking the trace of the matrix in eqn. (
8.18
) and using the explicit expressions for the Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-functions gives us the angular distribution of the decay products,
M_{2}
and
B^{\prime}
,

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})&=\frac{5}{8}(\rho_{11}(B_{3/2})+\rho_{-1-1}(B_{3/2}))(1+\frac{3}{5}\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{3}{4}(\rho_{33}(B_{3/2})+\rho_{-3-3}(B_{3/2}))\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Re[\rho_{31}(B_{3/2})-\rho_{-1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Im[\rho_{31}(B_{3/2})-\rho_{-1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Re[\rho_{3-1}(B_{3/2})+\rho_{1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Im[\rho_{3-1}(B_{3/2})+\rho_{1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}.\\
\end{split}

(8.20)

For the case of the reaction
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
, the same procedure just described applies. The only difference is that in this case the variables
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
describe different angles, because they describe the decay distribution of
M_{1}
and
B
instead of
M_{2}
and
B
.

As mentioned in section
3
, the coordinate system used to define the angles
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
is different for each of the three pathways shown in eqns. (
3.3
), (
3.4
) and (
3.5
). The
z^{\prime}
-axis is always defined as pointing in the opposite direction as the three-momentum of the spectator hadron, and the
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
are the polar and azimuthal angle of one of the decaying hadrons with respect to this axis, as shown in fig.
3.3
.

As we have seen in eqn. (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we can express spin density matrix elements in terms of statistical
tensors. In terms of these, the angular distribution for the decay of a
V
meson is given by

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}t_{00}(V)-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}t_{20}(V)(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\Re[t_{21}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}+\Im[t_{21}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Re[t_{22}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}-\Im[t_{22}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta\sin 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(8.21)

and the angular distribution for the decay of a
B_{3/2}
baryon is given by

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})&=\frac{1}{2}t_{00}(3/2)-\frac{1}{8}t_{20}(3/2)(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t_{21}(3/2)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t_{21}(3/2)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t_{22}(3/2)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t_{22}(3/2)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(8.22)

As can be seen from these last two equations, only statistical tensors of even rank appear in the expressions for the distribution (rank 1
tensors don’t appear in the expression for the distribution of a
V
meson, while rank 1 and 3 tensors don’t appear in the expression
for the distribution of the
B_{3/2}
baryon). As long as the decay process conserves parity, this property holds true even when interference effects of different resonances are taken into account, as we will show in section
12
.

Note how the functions of the angles multiplied by each of the tensors in the sum of the last two equations is a spherical harmonic,

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{3}}t_{00}(V)Y_{00}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})-2\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{15}}t_{20}(V)Y_{20}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}-4\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{15}}\Re[t_{21}(V)]\Re[Y_{21}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]+4\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{15}}\Im[t_{21}(V)]\Im[Y_{21}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}-4\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{15}}\Re[t_{22}(V)]\Re[Y_{22}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]+4\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{15}}\Im[t_{22}(V)]\Im[Y_{22}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})],\end{split}

(8.23)

and

\displaystyle W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\pi}t_{00}(3/2)Y_{00}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}t_{20}(3/2)Y_{20}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\Re[t_{21}(3/2)]\Re[Y_{21}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]+2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\Im[t_{21}(3/2)]\Im[Y_{21}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]

(8.24)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\Re[t_{22}(3/2)]\Re[Y_{22}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]+2\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\Im[t_{22}(3/2)]\Im[Y_{22}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})].

These last two equations show another advantage of using the statistical tensors instead of the SDME’s: the angular functions multiplied by the tensors of given
L
and
M
are proportional to the well known spherical harmonics with same
L
and
M
. By contrast, in the expressions in terms of the SDME’s in eqns. (
8.13
) and (
8.20
), the quantities multiplied by the angular functions are linear combinations of SDME’s, with no obvious connection between them and the angular function that multiply them. In section
12
we will show that this is in general true for any quasi-two-body state in which the decay is parity conserving.

Chapter 9
Polarization Observables in Terms of SDME’s and Statistical Tensors of the Quasi-Two-Body States

Given the assumption that the photoproduction process goes through a quasi-two-body state, the polarization observables can be expressed
in terms of the SDME’s or statistical tensors of the resonances that decay. We will first examine this for for pathway A, using the example of a decaying vector meson. Afterwards, we will examine this for pathways B and C for the example of a decaying spin-3/2 baryon.

We have shown in equation (
6.15
) how the spin density matrix of the final
M_{1}M_{2}B
system can be decomposed into a sum of 16 matrices. But this can be done for any spin density matrix, including the one for the vector meson V,

\hat{\rho}(V)=\rho^{0}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\hat{\rho}^{i(\gamma)}(V)+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}\hat{\rho}^{i(N)}(V)+\sum_{i,j}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}\hat{\rho}^{ij(\gamma N)}(V),

(9.1)

where the SDME’s of the 16 matrices are

\begin{split}\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}},\\
\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}\sigma^{j}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}.\end{split}

(9.2)

Note how the
\lambda_{B}
indices on the right-hand sides of the equations are summed over, since the partial trace was taken.
We can use the explicit forms of the Pauli matrices to simplify the expression further. If we label their elements with the integers
-1
and
1
for the first and second row/column index, respectively, we can write them in terms of the Kronecker delta,

\displaystyle\sigma^{x}_{m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,-m^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\sigma^{y}_{m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-im\delta_{m,-m^{\prime}},

(9.3)

\displaystyle\sigma^{z}_{m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-m\delta_{m,m^{\prime}},

\displaystyle m=\lambda_{\gamma}\quad

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad m=2\lambda_{N},

where
i
is the imaginary number. Examples of some of these matrices when the previous equation is applied are

\begin{split}&\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
&\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)=-\frac{i}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\gamma}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
&\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}(V)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\gamma}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}.\end{split}

(9.4)

By substituting eqn. (
9.1
) into eqn. (
8.12
), we see that the distribution
W
can also be decomposed into 16
terms,

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}W^{i(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i,j}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}W^{ij(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V).\end{split}

(9.5)

This last equation is valid not just for vector mesons, but for any kind of decaying particle. The
W^{i}
’s are given by

\begin{split}W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{0\lambda_{V}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)D^{1}_{0\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{i(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{0\lambda_{V}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)D^{1}_{0\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{0\lambda_{V}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)D^{1}_{0\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{ij(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}D^{1*}_{0\lambda_{V}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)D^{1}_{0\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).\\
\end{split}

(9.6)

Each of these
W^{i}
’s will have exactly the same mathematical form as
W
in eqn. (
8.13
) after the substitution

\rho_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}\rightarrow\rho^{i}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda^{\prime}_{V}}
. For example,
W^{y(\gamma)}
takes the form

\begin{split}W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{00}(V)\cos^{2}\theta^{*}+\frac{1}{2}(\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{11}(V)+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{-1-1}(V))\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Re[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{10}(V)-\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{0-1}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Im[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{10}(V)-\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{0-1}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Re[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-1}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}+\Im[\rho^{y(\gamma)(V)}_{1-1}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(9.7)

In eqn. (
7.42
) we showed how statistical tensors can also be decomposed into other 16 statistical tensors. The equations that relates the 16 SDME’s to the 16 tensors were shown in eqns. (
7.40
) and (
7.41
). To find the
W^{i}
’s in terms of these tensors, we simply substitute eqn. (
7.41
) into the definition of the
W^{i}
’s in eqn. (
9.6
) to get, for example,

\begin{split}W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}t^{y(\gamma)}_{00}(V)-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}t^{y(\gamma)}_{20}(V)(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}-\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}(V)]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}+\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}(V)]\sin^{2}\theta\sin 2\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(9.8)

Note how it has the same mathematical form as the the expression for
W
in terms of the tensors shown in eqn. (
8.21
) except that now the tensors have the appropriate superscript.

To find how these 16
W^{i}
’s are related to the polarization observables, we use eqn. (
7
) and take the trace on both sides. The terms proportional to the Pauli matrices,
\sigma^{i}
, on the right-hand side will vanish. The left-hand side is the definition of the distribution
W
,

W\equiv\Tr[\hat{\rho}(M_{1}M_{2}B)].

(9.9)

After taking said trace and comparing it with eqn. (
9.5
), we get

\displaystyle W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}\cos 2\beta I^{c}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}\sin 2\beta I^{s}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

(9.10)

\displaystyle W^{z(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}I^{\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}P_{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{ix(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}\cos 2\beta P_{i}^{c}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{iy(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}\sin 2\beta P_{i}^{s}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{iz(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)

\displaystyle=I_{0}P_{i}^{\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

This last equation is not only valid for the decay of vector mesons, but also for any type of decaying particle. In deriving these expressions, a sum over the spin indices of the recoil baryon baryon was done. This represents the fact that the polarization of the recoiling particle is not measured. As such, the
W^{i}
’s are related to the observables that do not involve the recoil baryon.

For the cases of pathways B and C with a decaying spin-3/2 baryon,
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{1}B
, and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{1}B
, we follow the same procedure and decompose the decaying baryon’s spin density matrix into 16 matrices,

\begin{split}\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2})&=\rho^{0}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\hat{\rho}^{i(\gamma)}(B_{3/2})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}\hat{\rho}^{i(N)}(B_{3/2})+\sum_{i,j}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}\hat{\rho}^{ij(\gamma N)}(B_{3/2}),\end{split}

(9.11)

where

\displaystyle\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}}}(B_{3/2})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M_{0\lambda_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{0\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},

\displaystyle\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}}}(B_{3/2})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\end{subarray}}M_{0\lambda_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{0\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}};\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},

(9.12)

\displaystyle\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}}}(B_{3/2})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\end{subarray}}M_{0\lambda_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}}M^{*}_{0\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}},

\displaystyle\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}}}(B_{3/2})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\end{subarray}}M_{0\lambda_{B_{3/2}};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}\sigma^{j}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{0\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}};\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}},

(compare with equation (
9.2
) for the vector meson V). The angular distribution of its decay products can again be decomposed into 16 terms,

\displaystyle W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})=W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}W^{i(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

(9.13)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i}\Lambda^{i(N)}W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})+\sum_{i,j}\Lambda^{i(\gamma)}\Lambda^{j(N)}W^{ij(N\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2}).

where

\displaystyle W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}D^{3/2*}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{i(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}D^{3/2*}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

(9.14)

\displaystyle W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}D^{3/2*}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{ij(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}D^{3/2*}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

(note the sum over the index
\lambda_{B}
, since we are taking a trace). Each of these
W^{i}
’s will have exactly the same mathematical form as
W
in eqn. (
8.20
) after the substitution
\rho_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})\rightarrow\rho^{i}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda^{\prime}_{B_{3/2}}}(B_{3/2})
. For example,
W^{y(\gamma)}
is given by

\displaystyle W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{8}(\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{11}(B_{3/2})+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{-1-1}(B_{3/2}))(1+\frac{3}{5}\cos 2\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{3}{4}(\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{33}(B_{3/2})+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{-3-3}(B_{3/2}))\sin^{2}\theta^{*}

(9.15)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Re[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{31}(B_{3/2})-\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{-1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Im[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{31}(B_{3/2})-\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{-1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Re[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{3-1}(B_{3/2})+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Im[\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{3-1}(B_{3/2})+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-3}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}.

In terms of statistical tensors, this distribution is

\displaystyle W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=t^{y(\gamma)}_{00}(B_{3/2})-\frac{1}{4}t^{y(\gamma)}_{20}(B_{3/2})(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}

(9.16)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}.

The polarization observables for this case are again given by eqn. (
9
),

\displaystyle W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}P^{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}\cos 2\beta I^{c}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}\sin 2\beta I^{s}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

(9.17)

\displaystyle W^{z(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}I^{\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{ix(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}\cos 2\beta P^{ic}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{iy(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}\sin 2\beta P^{is}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),

\displaystyle W^{iz(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})

\displaystyle=I_{0}P^{i\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

Note that while the dependence on the scattering angle
\theta
is contained inside of the SDME’s or statistical tensors of the decaying hadron, their
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
dependence is not. Rather, they are fully contained in the trigonometric functions in
eqns. (
9.7
), (
9
), (
9
) and (
9
). For the expressions involving the SDME’s, these trigonometric functions are made up of two multiplied elements of Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-functions. For those involving the statistical tensors, the trigonometric functions are spherical harmonics. Since the dependence of the distribution and observables on these angles is known, the expressions derived here can be used to apply fits to the data with the SDME’s or statistical tensors as fit parameters. Note, however, that so far we have made the assumption that only one quasi-two-body state process contributes to the process. We will later consider the case of multiple channels contributing.

Chapter 10
Parity Invariance Considerations

Parity is conserved in electromagnetic and strong interactions. Therefore, the transition amplitudes in the reactions we
are discussing will be invariant under parity transformations (up to a phase factor).

For the case of two body scattering such as
\pi N\rightarrow MB
(where
M
is a pseudoscalar meson and
B
is a baryon), parity conservation leads to

M_{-\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{N}}(\theta)=(-1)^{\lambda_{N}-\lambda_{B}}M_{\lambda_{B};\lambda_{N}}(\theta),

(10.1)

and reduces the number of independent transition amplitudes from 4 to 2.

For a three body scattering process such as
\pi N\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
(where both
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are pseudoscalar mesons) the relationship is

M_{-\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{N}}(\theta;\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=(-1)^{\lambda_{N}-\lambda_{B}}M_{\lambda_{B};\lambda_{N}}(\theta;\theta^{*},2\pi-\phi^{*}).

(10.2)

This relates two amplitudes at different kinematic points. Therefore, for the case of a 3-body final state, parity conservation
cannot be used to reduce the number of independent amplitudes at a single kinematic point.
For
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
we have

M_{-\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}}(\theta;\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=(-1)^{\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}+\lambda_{B}}M_{\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}(\theta;\theta^{*},2\pi-\phi^{*}).

(10.3)

For the general case of two-body scattering with arbitrary spin,
A\ B\rightarrow a\ b
, the relations are

M_{-\lambda_{a}-\lambda_{b};-\lambda_{A}-\lambda_{B}}(\theta)=\frac{\eta_{a}\eta_{b}}{\eta_{A}\eta_{B}}(-1)^{(\lambda_{b}-\lambda_{a})-(\lambda_{B}-\lambda_{A})}M_{\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b};\lambda_{A}\lambda_{B}}(\theta),

(10.4)

where the
\lambda
’s and
\eta
’s are the helicities and intrinsic parities of the particles, respectively.

Since we are assuming in this thesis that the photoproduction reaction occurs through a quasi-two-body state, we can apply this last
equation to the reactions
\gamma N\rightarrow VB
,
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2}
and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B_{3/2}
,

\begin{split}M_{-\lambda_{V}-\lambda_{B};-\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}}(\theta)&=(-1)^{(\lambda_{V}-\lambda_{B})-(\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N})}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}(\theta),\\
M_{0-\lambda_{B_{3/2}};-\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}}(\theta)&=(-1)^{-\lambda_{B_{3/2}}-(\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N})}M_{0\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}(\theta).\end{split}

(10.5)

For the
V
meson, these relations can be used with eqn. (
9.4
) in order to give

\begin{split}&\rho^{0}_{-\lambda_{V},-\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)=(-1)^{\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V),\\
&\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{-\lambda_{V},-\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)=\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(V)(-1)^{\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V),\\
&\rho^{i(N)}_{-\lambda_{V},-\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)=\zeta^{(N)i}(V)(-1)^{\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V),\\
&\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{-\lambda_{V},-\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V)=\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(V)\zeta^{(N)j}(V)(-1)^{\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{V},\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}(V),\\
&\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(V)=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1&i=y,z,\\
-1&i=x,\end{cases}\\
&\zeta^{(N)i}(V)=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1&i=x,\\
-1&i=y,z.\end{cases}\end{split}

(10.6)

The values for the
\zeta^{i(\gamma)}(V)
’s and
\zeta^{i(N)}(V)
’s are due to the values of the intrinsic parities of the particles
involved in the interaction and from the fact that
(-1)^{2\lambda_{\gamma}}=1
,
(-1)^{2\lambda_{N}}=-1
, and
(-1)^{2\lambda_{B}}=-1

for all
\lambda_{\gamma}
(integer),
\lambda_{N}
(half-integer), and
\lambda_{B}
(half-integer).
For the
B_{3/2}
baryon, applying these parity relations to eqn. (
9
) gives

\displaystyle\rho^{0}_{-\lambda_{B_{3/2}},-\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})=(-1)^{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}+\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}\rho^{0}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2}),

\displaystyle\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{-\lambda_{B_{3/2}},-\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})=\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(B_{3/2})(-1)^{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}+\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2}),

\displaystyle\rho^{i(N)}_{-\lambda_{B_{3/2}},-\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})=\zeta^{(N)i}(B_{3/2})(-1)^{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}+\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2}),

(10.7)

\displaystyle\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{-\lambda_{B_{3/2}},-\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2})=\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(B_{3/2})\zeta^{(N)j}(B_{3/2})(-1)^{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}+\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}(B_{3/2}),

\displaystyle\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(B_{3/2})=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1&i=x,\\
-1&i=y,z,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\zeta^{(N)i}(B_{3/2})=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1&i=y,z\\
-1&i=x.\end{cases}

Note that for the case of the
B_{3/2}
baryon, the values of the

\zeta^{i}
’s are different from those of the
V
meson by a factor of
-1
. This is because the derivation does not involve the
quantity
(-1)^{2\lambda_{B}}
since the hadron in the quasi-two-body state that accompanies the baryon
B_{3/2}
is spinless. We therefore conclude that, aside from the factor of
(-1)^{\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{V}^{\prime}}
in the case of the
V

meson and a factor of
(-1)^{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}+\lambda_{B_{3/2}}^{\prime}}
in the case of the
B_{3/2}
baryon, 8 of the 16 matrices will get an
extra factor of
-1
in these parity relations. It is this extra factor of
-1
that will have a very specific consequence in using these
relations to simplify the expressions for the
W^{i}
’s in eqns. (
9.6
) and (
9
).

While eqns. (
10.6
) and (
10
) were derived for the case of a decaying vector meson and a decaying spin-3/2 baryon, respectively, the values of the
\zeta
factors in eqn. (
10.6
) will be the same for any meson of any spin and intrinsic parity, while those in eqn. (
10
) will be the same for any baryon of any spin and intrinsic parity. The reason it is independent of intrinsic parity is because when the parity relations in eqn. (
10.4
) are applied in eqn. (
6.8
), each of the two transition matrices give you one factor of the same intrinsic parity. Since the only possible values are
1
and
-1
, the overall factor will always be
1
regardless of intrinsic parity. The
\zeta
factor also depends on whether the decaying resonance is a baryon or meson, but not on its spin. For a quasi-two-body state consisting of a meson and a baryon of arbitrary spins
MB
, if the meson decays the derivation gives you factors of
(-1)^{2\lambda_{B}}
. But since
\lambda_{B}
is a multiple of 1/2, this factor will always be
-1
regardless of the spin. If it is the baryon that decays, the derivation instead gives you a factors of
(-1)^{2\lambda_{M}}
. But since
\lambda_{M}
is a multiple of 1, this factor will always be 1 regardless of the spin.

Using eqn. (
5.7
) along with the parity relations in eqns. (
10.6
) and (
10
) leads to

t^{i}_{LM}=\zeta^{i}(-1)^{L+M+2S}t^{i}_{L-M},

(10.8)

for the statistical tensors.

The constraint of parity invariance in the production reaction will reduce the total number of independent parameters that describe the 16
matrices in eqns. (
9.1
) and (
9.11
), which means the expressions for the
W^{i}
’s will
simplify. Take for example the expression for
W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)
in eqn. (
9.7
). By using the relations in eqn. (
10.6
) and substituting into these equations, we see that all of the terms that are proportional to sine
functions of
\phi^{*}
vanish. Since
\phi^{*}
then only appears inside of cosine functions,
W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)
is an even function of
\phi^{*}
,

\displaystyle W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)=

\displaystyle\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{11}(V)\sin^{2}\theta^{*}+\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{00}(V)\cos^{2}\theta^{*}

(10.9)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\Re\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}

(10.10)

\displaystyle-\Re\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-1}(V)\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}

(the parity relations imply that
\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-1}(V)
is purely real). We can also see that, for the case of

W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})
, it is the terms proportional to cosine functions of
\phi^{*}
that vanish (as well as the
first two terms). Since the
\phi^{*}
then only appears inside of sine functions,
W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)
is an odd function of
\phi^{*}
,

\displaystyle W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\Im\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}

(10.11)

\displaystyle+\Im\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{1-1}(V)\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*}

(the parity relations imply that
\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{1-1}(V)
is purely imaginary). We get the same results, as we should, if we express the

W^{i}
’s in terms of statistical tensors instead. By using the parity and hermiticity relations of the tensors, eqns. (
10.8
) and
(
5.10
), in eqn. (
9.8
), we get the following simplified expression,

\begin{split}W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}t^{y(\gamma)}_{00}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}t^{y(\gamma)}_{20}(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Re[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(10.12)

(parity invariance and hermiticity implies that
t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}
and
t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}
are purely real). In the same way,

W^{x(\gamma)}
in terms of the statistical tensors is simplified when parity is conserved,

\begin{split}W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};V)&=-\Im[t^{x(\gamma)}_{21}]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\Im[t^{x(\gamma)}_{22}]\sin^{2}\theta\sin 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(10.13)

(parity invariance and hermiticity implies that
t^{x(\gamma)}_{21}
and
t^{x(\gamma)}_{22}
are purely imaginary, while

t^{x(\gamma)}_{00}
and
t^{x(\gamma)}_{20}
are equal to zero).

For the case of the
B_{3/2}
baryon, it is

W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})
that is an odd function of
\phi^{*}
, while it is

W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})
that is an even function of
\phi^{*}
,

\begin{split}W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})=&\sqrt{2}\Im\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{10}(B_{3/2})\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&+\Im\rho^{y(\gamma)}_{1-1}(B_{3/2})\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(10.14)

\begin{split}W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})=&\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{11}(B_{3/2})\sin^{2}\theta^{*}+\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{00}(B_{3/2})\cos^{2}\theta^{*}\\
&-\sqrt{2}\Re\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(B_{3/2})\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&-\Re\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{1-1}(B_{3/2})\sin^{2}\theta\cos 2\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(10.15)

In terms of statistical tensors (again, by using the parity and hermiticity relations, eqns. (
10.8
) and (
5.10
)),

\begin{split}W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})&=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{21}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\sin\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Im[t^{y(\gamma)}_{22}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\sin 2\phi^{*},\end{split}

(10.16)

and

\begin{split}W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};B_{3/2})&=t^{x(\gamma)}_{00}(B_{3/2})-\frac{1}{4}t^{x(\gamma)}_{20}(B_{3/2})(1+3\cos 2\theta^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t^{x(\gamma)}_{21}(B_{3/2})]\sin 2\theta^{*}\cos\phi^{*}\\
&\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\Re[t^{x(\gamma)}_{22}(B_{3/2})]\sin^{2}\theta^{*}\cos 2\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(10.17)

We will later show that it is in general true that once the parity constraints on the production reaction are applied, each of the
W^{i}
’s
and, therefore, the observables, will be either even or odd functions of
\phi^{*}
.

Chapter 11
Angular Distributions and Polarization Observables When More Than One Quasi-Two-Body State Channel Contributes

11.1
Meson Interference

To examine the case when more than one quasi-two-body state contributes to the photoproduction reaction, we will first examine the example of three specific channels,

\begin{split}\gamma N\rightarrow SB\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B,\\
\gamma N\rightarrow VB\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B,\\
\gamma N\rightarrow TB\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B,\end{split}

(11.1)

where
S
,
V
and
T
are scalar, vector, and tensor (spin-
2
) mesons.

The main difference from the single-channel case is that in the multi-channel case the
\gamma N
state transitions into a linear
superposition of the
SB
,
VB
, and
TB
states,

\ket{\Psi(SB,VB,TB)}=\ket{\Psi(SB)}+\ket{\Psi(VB)}+\ket{\Psi(TB)}.

(11.2)

In matrix form, this is

\ket{\Psi(BS,BV,BT)}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0\frac{1}{2}}(SB)\\
\psi_{0\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(SB)\\
\psi_{1\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi_{1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi_{0\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}1\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(VB)\\
\psi_{2\frac{1}{2}}(TB)\\
\psi_{2-\frac{1}{2}}(TB)\\
\psi_{1\frac{1}{2}}(TB)\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{\scalebox{0.75}{-}2\scalebox{0.75}{-}\frac{1}{2}}(TB)\end{pmatrix},

(11.3)

where
\psi_{\lambda_{S}\lambda_{B}}(SB)
is the probability amplitude for the state in the ensemble to be found in a

SB
state with respective spin projections
\lambda_{S}
and
\lambda_{B}
, and similarly for

\psi_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B}}(VB)
and
\psi_{\lambda_{T}\lambda_{B}(TB)}
. The first
2
entries in this vector
are the amplitudes corresponding to the
BS
state, the next
6
correspond to the
BV
state, and the last
10

correspond to the
BT
state, making its Hilbert space
18
-dimensional. Its spin density matrix will therefore be
represented by a
18\times 4
matrix.

\hat{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\scriptscriptstyle M_{0\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{0-\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0-\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0-\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{0-\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(SB;\gamma N)\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{1\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{1-\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1-\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1-\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{1-\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\vdots\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{-1-\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-1-\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-1-\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-1-\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(VB;\gamma N)\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{2\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{2-\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2-\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2-\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{2-\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\vdots\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\scriptscriptstyle M_{-2-\frac{1}{2};1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-2-\frac{1}{2};1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-2-\frac{1}{2};-1\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)&\scriptscriptstyle M_{-2-\frac{1}{2};-1-\frac{1}{2}}(TB;\gamma N)\end{pmatrix}.

(11.4)

In the previous matrix, the first two rows describe the transition of the initial system into the
SB
system (first two entries in state vector in eqn. (
11.3
)). The next six rows describe the transition into the
VB
system (next six entries in the state vector), and the last ten rows describe the transition into the
TB
system (last ten entries in the state vector). Since this state vector is
18\times 1
, its density matrix will be
18\times 18
.

As with the single-channel case, the initial and final density matrices are related by eqn. (
6.8
). But this time, since the indices in the transition matrix in eqn. (
11.4
) refer to different channels, we will express the resulting spin density matrix in terms of submatrices,

\hat{\rho}(S,\ V,\ T)=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\rho}(S)&\hat{\xi}(S,V)&\hat{\eta}(S,T)\\
\hat{\xi}^{{\dagger}}(V,S)&\hat{\rho}(V)&\hat{\chi}(V,T)\\
\hat{\eta}^{{\dagger}}(T,S)&\hat{\chi}^{{\dagger}}(T,V)&\hat{\rho}(T)\end{pmatrix}.

(11.5)

The diagonal matrices are the ones that are obtained when the reaction is single-channel and no interference takes place. We will refer to the off-diagonal submatrices as interference matrices. We can also write it as
