\hat{\rho}(S,V,T)=\hat{\rho}(S)+\hat{\rho}(V)+\hat{\rho}(T)+\big[\hat{\xi}(S,V)+\hat{\eta}(S,T)+\hat{\chi}(V,T)+\text{h.c.}\big],

(11.6)

where the matrix elements of the interference matrices are

\begin{split}\xi(S,V)_{0\lambda_{V}}\equiv&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{0\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\rho(\gamma N)_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}}M^{*}_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}},\\
\eta(S,T)_{0\lambda_{T}}\equiv&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{0\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\rho(\gamma N)_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}}M^{*}_{\lambda_{T}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}},\\
\chi(V,T)_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{T}}\equiv&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{B}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}\rho(\gamma N)_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}}M^{*}_{\lambda_{T}\lambda_{B};\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}^{\prime}},\end{split}

(11.7)

(note how there is a sum over
\lambda_{B}
, indicating that the partial trace over the recoil baryon has been taken). These interference matrices therefore have “mixed” indices: their “row” and “column” indices refer to a different meson. Since these mesons have different spin, these matrices are not square:
\hat{\xi}
is
1\times 3
,
\hat{\eta}
is
1\times 5
, and
\hat{\chi}
is
3\times 5
.

We now proceed to find the angular distribution for the decay of the meson into
M_{1}M_{2}
,

W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};S,V,B)=\hat{\cal{D}}^{\dagger}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\hat{\rho}(S,V,T)\hat{\cal{D}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

(11.8)

This time, the form of the transition matrix
\hat{\cal{D}}^{\dagger}
is

\hat{\cal{D}}=\begin{pmatrix}D^{0}_{00}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{1}_{01}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{1}_{00}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{1}_{0-1}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{2}_{02}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
D^{2}_{01}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
\vdots\\
D^{2}_{0-2}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\end{pmatrix}.

(11.9)

The distribution can be written as

\displaystyle W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};S,V,T)

\displaystyle=W^{(S)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(V)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(T)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(SV)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+W^{(ST)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(TV)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

(11.10)

In this expression
W^{(S)}
is the contribution of a scalar intermediate meson,
W^{(V)}
arise from a vector meson, and
W^{(T)}
arises from a meson of spin 2.
W^{(SV)}
arises from the interference between a scalar and vector meson,
W^{(ST)}
comes from the interference between a scalar and a tensor, and
W^{(TV)}
arises from interference between a vector and tensor meson. The explicit forms are

W^{(S)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=\rho_{00}(S),

(11.11)

\displaystyle W^{(V)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{00}(V)}{2}+\frac{\rho_{11}(V)+\rho_{-1-1}(V)}{4}+\bigg(\frac{\rho_{00}(V)}{2}-\frac{\rho_{11}(V)+\rho_{-1-1}(V)}{4}\bigg)\cos(2\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\Im[\rho_{1-1}(V)]\sin(2\phi^{*})-\Re[\rho_{1-1}(V)]\cos(2\phi^{*})\bigg)\bigg(1-\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)

(11.12)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sin(2\theta^{*})}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\Re[\rho_{0-1}(V)-\rho_{10}(V)]\cos(\phi^{*})+\Im[\rho_{10}(V)-\rho_{0-1}(V)]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg),

\displaystyle W^{(T)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{11+12\cos(2\theta^{*})+9\cos(4\theta^{*})}{32}\rho_{00}(T)+\bigg(1-\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\frac{3}{16}\big(\rho_{11}(T)+\rho_{-1-1}(T)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{3}{8}\Big(\Re[\rho_{1-1}(T)]\cos(2\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{1-1}(T)]\sin(2\phi^{*})\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{3}{64}\bigg(\rho_{22}(T)+\rho_{-2-2}(T)\bigg)\bigg(3-4\cos(2\theta^{*})+\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(\Re[\rho_{0-2}(T)+\rho_{20}(T)]\cos(2\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{0-2}(T)+\rho_{20}(T)]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg)\bigg(3\cos(4\theta^{*})-4\cos(2\theta^{*})+1\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{3}{32}\bigg(\Re[\rho_{2-2}(T)]\cos(4\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{2-2}(T)]\sin(4\phi^{*})\bigg)\bigg(3-4\cos(2\theta^{*})+\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(\Re[\rho_{0-1}(T)-\rho_{10}(T)]\cos(2\phi^{*})

(11.13)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{0-1}(T)-\rho_{10}(T)]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg)\bigg(2\sin(2\theta^{*})+3\sin(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{3}{16}\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{2-1}(T)-\rho_{1-2}(T)]\cos(3\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{2-1}(T)-\rho_{1-2}(T)]\sin(3\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(\Re[\rho_{-1-2}(T)-\rho_{21}(T)]\cos(\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{-1-2}(T)-\rho_{21}(T)]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\times\bigg(2\sin(2\theta^{*})-\sin(4\theta^{*})\bigg),

\displaystyle W^{(SV)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=2\Re[\xi_{00}]\cos(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sqrt{2}\sin(\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re[\xi_{0-1}-\xi_{01}]\cos(\phi^{*})-\Im[\xi_{01}+\xi_{0-1}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg),

(11.14)

\displaystyle W^{(ST)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Re[\eta_{00}]\bigg(1+3\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\sin(2\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re[\eta_{0-1}-\eta_{01}]\cos(\phi^{*})-\Im[\eta_{01}+\eta_{0-1}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

(11.15)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(1-\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\eta_{02}+\eta_{0-2}]\cos(2\phi^{*})+\Im[\eta_{02}-\eta_{0-2}]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg),

and

\displaystyle W^{(VT)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Re[\chi_{00}](5\cos(\theta^{*})+3\cos(3\theta^{*}))

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+(\cos(\theta^{*})-\cos(3\theta^{*}))\Bigg\{\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\bigg(\Re[\chi_{11}+\chi_{-1-1}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\Re[\chi_{-11}-\chi_{1-1}]\cos(2\phi^{*})-\Im[\chi_{-11}+\chi_{1-1}]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(\Re[\chi_{02}+\chi_{0-2}]\cos(2\phi^{*})-\Im[\chi_{02}-\chi_{0-2}]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\bigg(3\sin(3\theta^{*})-\sin(\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{10}-\chi_{-10}]\cos(\phi^{*}))

(11.16)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\Im[\chi_{10}+\chi_{-10}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg(\sin(\theta^{*})+\sin(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{0-1}-\chi_{01}]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\chi_{01}+\chi_{0-1}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8}\bigg(3\sin(\theta^{*})-\sin(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{-12}-\chi_{1-2}]\cos(3\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\Im[\chi_{-12}+\chi_{1-2}]\sin(3\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\Re[\chi_{-1-2}-\chi_{12}]\cos(\phi^{*})-\Im[\chi_{-1-2}+\chi_{12}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg).

11.2
Baryon Interference

We will now examine the case for the decay of photoproduced baryons in the reactions
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
and
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{2}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B
under the assumption that three different baryons in the intermediate quasi-two-body state
contribute of spins
1/2
,
3/2
, and
5/2
.

Just like in the case of the angular distribution of decaying mesons, the SDME’s of the intermediate quasi-two-body
state, which is a linear superposition of the three baryons that contribute, can be grouped in to different blocks:

\hat{\rho}(S)=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\rho}(1/2)&\hat{\xi}&\hat{\eta}\\
\hat{\xi}^{{\dagger}}&\hat{\rho}(3/2)&\hat{\chi}\\
\hat{\eta}^{{\dagger}}&\hat{\chi}^{{\dagger}}&\hat{\rho}(5/2)\end{pmatrix}.

(11.17)

\hat{\rho}(1/2)
,
\hat{\rho}(3/2)
, and
\hat{\rho}(5/2)
are the spin density matrices that the spin-
1/2
,

3/2
, and
5/2
baryons would have if they were contributing to the process by themselves. The indices of these three matrices
only correspond to one hadron (the spin-
1/2
,
3/2
, and
5/2
baryons). The rest of the blocks have mixed indices:
one index corresponds to one hadron and the other to a different hadron. The left index of the matrix
\hat{\xi}
corresponds to the
spin-1/2 baryon, while its right index corresponds to the spin-3/2 baryon. For the matrix
\hat{\eta}
, the left index
corresponds to the spin-1/2 baryon and the right index to the spin-5/2 baryon. Finally, for the matrix
\hat{\chi}

the left index corresponds to the spin-3/2 baryon and the right index corresponds to the spin-5/2 baryons.
The matrices with the daggers are the conjugate transpose of their respective matrix.

The distribution can be written as

\displaystyle W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};1/2,3/2,5/2)

\displaystyle=W^{(1/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(3/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+W^{(1/2,3/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(1/2,5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+W^{(3/2,5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).

(11.18)

In this expression
W^{(1/2)}
is the contribution of a spin-
1/2
intermediate baryon,
W^{(3/2)}
arises from a baryon with spin 3/2, and
W^{(5/2)}
arises from a baryon with spin 5/2.
W^{(1/2,3/2)}
arises from the interference between the baryons with spin 1/2 and 3/2,
W^{(1/2,5/2)}
comes from the interference between baryons with spin 1/2 and spin 5/2, and
W^{(3/2,5/2)}
arises from interference between baryons with spin 3/2 and spin 5/2. The explicit forms are

\displaystyle W^{(1/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=\rho_{11}(1/2)+\rho_{-1-1}(1/2),

(11.19)

\displaystyle W^{(3/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\Bigg\{3\bigg(1-\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\rho_{33}(3/2)+\rho_{-3-3}(3/2)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(5+3\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\rho_{11}(3/2)+\rho_{-1-1}(3/2)\bigg)\Bigg\}

(11.20)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\bigg(1-\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{1-3}(3/2)+\rho_{3-1}(3/2)]\cos(2\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{1-3}(3/2)+\rho_{3-1}(3/2)]\sin(2\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\sin(2\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re[\rho_{-1-3}(3/2)-\rho_{31}(3/2)]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{-1-3}(3/2)-\rho_{31}(3/2)]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg),

\displaystyle W^{(5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32}\bigg(\rho_{11}(5/2)+\rho_{-1-1}(5/2)\bigg)\bigg(12+15\cos(2\theta^{*})+5\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{64}\bigg(\rho_{33}(5/2)+\rho_{-3-3}(5/2)\bigg)\bigg(19-4\cos(2\theta^{*})-15\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{5}{64}\bigg(\rho_{55}(5/2)+\rho_{-5-5}(5/2)\bigg)\bigg(3-4\cos(2\theta^{*})+\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{8}\bigg(2\sin(2\theta^{*})-\sin(4\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\times\Bigg\{\Re[\rho_{-3-5}(5/2)-\rho_{53}(5/2)]\cos(\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{-3-5}(5/2)-\rho_{53}(5/2)]\sin(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg(\Re[\rho_{5-1}(5/2)-\rho_{1-5}(5/2)]\cos(3\phi^{*})-\Im[\rho_{5-1}(5/2)-\rho_{1-5}(5/2)]\sin(3\phi^{*})\bigg)\Bigg\}

(11.21)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{32}\bigg(3-4\cos(2\theta^{*})+\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{5-3}(5/2)+\rho_{3-5}(5/2)]\cos(4\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{5-3}(5/2)+\rho_{3-5}(5/2)]\sin(4\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}}\bigg(-1+4\cos(2\theta^{*})-3\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{51}(5/2)+\rho_{-1-5}(5/2)]\cos(4\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{51}(5/2)+\rho_{-1-5}(5/2)]\sin(4\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{8\sqrt{2}}\bigg(6\sin(2\theta^{*})-5\sin(4\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{-1-3}(5/2)-\rho_{31}(5/2)]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{-1-3}(5/2)-\rho_{31}(5/2)]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{16\sqrt{2}}\bigg(9-4\cos(2\theta^{*})-5\cos(4\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\rho_{1-3}(5/2)-\rho_{3-1}(5/2)]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{1-3}(5/2)-\rho_{3-1}(5/2)]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg),

\displaystyle W^{(1/2,3/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=2\Re[\xi_{11}+\xi_{-1-1}]\cos(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sin(\theta^{*})\Bigg\{\bigg(\Re[\xi_{1-1}-\xi_{-11}]+\sqrt{3}\Re[\xi_{-1-3}-\xi_{13}]\bigg)\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\Im[\xi_{1-1}+\xi_{-11}]+\sqrt{3}\Im[\xi_{-1-3}+\xi_{13}]\bigg)\sin(\phi^{*})\Bigg\},

(11.22)

\displaystyle W^{(1/2,5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Re[\eta_{11}+\eta_{-1-1}]\bigg(1+3\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\bigg(1-\cos(2\theta^{*})\bigg)\Bigg\{\bigg(\Re[\eta_{1-3}+\eta_{-13}]+\sqrt{5}\Re[\eta_{15}+\eta_{-1-5}]\bigg)\cos(2\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\bigg(\Im[\eta_{-13}-\eta_{1-3}]+\sqrt{5}\Im[\eta_{15}-\eta_{-1-5}]\bigg)\sin(2\phi^{*})\Bigg\}

(11.23)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sin(2\theta^{*})\Bigg\{\bigg(\Re[\eta_{1-1}-\eta_{-11}]+\sqrt{2}\Re[\eta_{-1-3}-\eta_{13}]\bigg)\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\Im[\eta_{1-1}+\eta_{-11}]+\sqrt{2}\Im[\eta_{-1-3}+\eta_{13}]\bigg)\sin(\phi^{*})\Bigg\},

and

\displaystyle W^{(3/2,5/2)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\sqrt{2}}\bigg(3\sin(\theta^{*})-\sin(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\Bigg\{\sqrt{15}\bigg(\Re[\chi_{-3-5}-\chi_{35}]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\chi_{-3-5}+\chi_{35}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(\sqrt{5}\Re[\chi_{-15}-\chi_{1-5}]+\sqrt{3}\Re[\chi_{-33}-\chi_{3-3}]\bigg)\cos(3\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(\sqrt{5}\Im[\chi_{-15}+\chi_{1-5}]+\sqrt{3}\Im[\chi_{-33}+\chi_{3-3}]\bigg)\sin(3\phi^{*})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{8}\bigg(\cos(\theta^{*})-\cos(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\Bigg\{2\sqrt{6}\Re[\chi_{33}+\chi_{-3-3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(\sqrt{10}\Re[\chi_{15}+\chi_{-1-5}]-2\sqrt{3}\Re[\chi_{3-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\chi_{-31}]+\sqrt{3}\Re[\chi_{-13}+\chi_{1-3}]\bigg)\cos(2\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\bigg(\sqrt{10}\Im[\chi_{15}-\chi_{-1-5}]-2\sqrt{3}\Im[\chi_{3-1}-\chi_{-31}]

(11.24)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sqrt{3}\Im[\chi_{-13}-\chi_{1-3}]\bigg)\sin(2\phi^{*})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(3\cos(\theta^{*})+\cos(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\Re[\chi_{11}+\chi_{-1-1}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{8}\bigg(5\sin(\theta^{*})+\sin(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{1-1}-\chi_{-11}]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\chi_{1-1}+\chi_{-11}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{1}{8\sqrt{2}}\bigg(11\sin(\theta^{*})+7\sin(3\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{-1-3}-\chi_{13}]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}-\Im[\chi_{-1-3}+\chi_{13}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{8}\bigg(3\sin(3\theta^{*})-7\sin(\theta^{*})\bigg)\bigg(\Re[\chi_{-3-1}-\chi_{31}]\cos(\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\Im[\chi_{-3-1}+\chi_{31}]\sin(\phi^{*})\bigg).

There is one important aspect to consider in the case of decaying intermediate state baryons. Unlike the case of a decaying meson, if
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are the same meson, then an individual baryon resonance has two possible decay channels:
B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}B
, and
B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{2}B
. This means that the decay should be considered as an interference of two different states, each with its own individual spin density
matrix. Therefore, the density matrix of a single decaying baryon will have this form,

\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2})=\begin{pmatrix}&\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2}^{\text{B}})&\hat{\xi}(B_{3/2}^{\text{B}},B_{3/2}^{\text{C}})\\
&\hat{\xi}^{\dagger}(B_{3/2}^{\text{B}},B_{3/2}^{\text{C}})&\hat{\rho}(B_{3/2}^{\text{C}})\end{pmatrix}.

(11.25)

The diagonal spin density submatrices describe the spin state of the baryon resonance in pathways B and C. The off-diagonal submatrices is the interference matrix between the same baryon in pathways B and C.

11.3
Mesons and Baryon Interference

Only minor modifications are needed for the case of interference of intermediate quasi-two-body states where some of them contain decaying
mesons and some of them contain decaying baryons. To illustrate this, we will use as an example the case where two states interfere: one
intermediate state has a decaying spin-1 meson
V
, and the other has a decaying spin-3/2 baryon
B_{3/2}
,

\begin{split}\gamma N\rightarrow VB\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B,\\
\gamma N\rightarrow M_{1}B_{3/2}\rightarrow M_{1}M_{2}B\end{split}

(11.26)

(for simplicity, we will only consider pathways A and B). In the distribution, there will be a set of terms that contain the SDME’s of the interference submatrix
\hat{\xi}
,

W(V,B_{3/2})=\sum_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}D^{1*}_{0\lambda_{V}}(\theta^{*}_{\text{A}},\phi^{*}_{\text{A}})\xi_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};0\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{\text{B}},\phi^{*}_{\text{B}}).

(11.27)

Note that the angles in the arguments of the two Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
are different. This is due to the fact that the angles for the two
decaying resonances are defined in terms of different coordinate systems. Expressions relating these different angles will be given in appendix
A
.

In the cases in which only mesons or only baryons interfered, we could take the partial trace over the spectator hadron to get the spin density matrix of the decaying hadron.
But in this case, the helicity indices of the interference matrix have different spectator hadrons in the “row” and “column” indices: the baryon
B
and the meson
M_{1}
(since
M_{1}
is spinless, its helicity index is equal to
0
). Therefore, we cannot define a partial trace on this interference matrix since the
\lambda_{B}
helicity index
appears on both this matrix and on one of the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions. In this case, one option is to write the expression for the distribution in terms of this matrix without taking the partial trace. Another is to decompose the matrix into a sum of spin density matrices for the
VB
system into eigenstates of total spin, which can take on values of
1/2
or
3/2
in this case,

\displaystyle\xi_{\lambda_{V}\lambda_{B};0\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\widetilde{S},\widetilde{M}}C^{\widetilde{S}\widetilde{M}}_{1\lambda_{V};\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{B}}\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\widetilde{S},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}

\displaystyle=C^{\frac{1}{2}\ (\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{B})}_{1\lambda_{V};\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{B}}\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{(\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{B})\ \lambda_{B_{3/2}}}

(11.28)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\ \ C^{\frac{3}{2}\ (\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{B})}_{1\lambda_{V};\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{B}}\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{(\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{B})\ \lambda_{B_{3/2}}}.

In the previous expression,
\hat{\widetilde{\xi}}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\big)
and
\hat{\widetilde{\xi}}\big(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\big)
are interference spin density matrices between
B_{3/2}
and the
VB
system in its
S=1/2
eigenstate, and between
B_{3/2}
and the
VB
system in its
S=3/2
eigenstate. The left index on these density matrices labels the total spin projection of the
VB
system (which is set to
\widetilde{M}=\lambda_{V}+\lambda_{B}
from conservation of angular momentum), while the right index labels the spin projection of the
B_{3/2}
baryon. The expression for the distribution in equation
will therefore contain terms of this form,

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\cdots+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\\
\lambda_{B}\lambda_{V}\end{subarray}}D^{*1}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*}_{A},\phi^{*}_{A})C^{1/2\widetilde{M}}_{1\lambda_{V};1/2\lambda_{B}}\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{B},\phi^{*}_{B})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\hphantom{\cdots}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\\
\lambda_{B}\lambda_{V}\end{subarray}}D^{*1}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*}_{A},\phi^{*}_{A})C^{3/2\widetilde{M}}_{1\lambda_{V};1/2\lambda_{B}}\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{B},\phi^{*}_{B})+\cdots

(11.29)

\displaystyle=\cdots+\sum_{\widetilde{M},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}\widetilde{D}^{1/2*}_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{A},\phi^{*}_{A})\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{B},\phi^{*}_{B})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}+\sum_{\widetilde{M},\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}\widetilde{D}^{3/2*}_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{A},\phi^{*}_{A})\widetilde{\xi}\bigg(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\bigg)_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B_{3/2}}}D^{3/2}_{\lambda_{B_{3/2}}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*}_{B},\phi^{*}_{B})+\cdots,

where

\widetilde{D}^{\widetilde{S}*}_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{B}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\equiv\sum_{\lambda_{V}}D^{*1}_{\lambda_{V}0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})C^{\widetilde{S}\widetilde{M}}_{1/2\lambda_{B},1\lambda_{V}}\quad\text{with}\ \widetilde{S}=1/2,\ 3/2.

(11.30)

The quantity defined in the previous equation can be considered a generalized Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
function, and also a decay
amplitudes for the
\widetilde{S}=1/2
and
\widetilde{S}=3/2
component or the
VB
system to decay into
B
. Using the following properties of the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}

functions and of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients,

\displaystyle D^{*S}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})}D^{S}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

\displaystyle C^{c\gamma}_{a\alpha;b\beta}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a-\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{2c+1}{2b+1}}C^{b\beta}_{c\gamma;a-\alpha},

(11.31)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta,

the following property can be shown,

\begin{split}\widetilde{D}^{*\widetilde{S}}_{-\widetilde{M}-\lambda_{N}}&=(-1)^{\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{M}+S_{R}+\frac{1}{2}-\lambda_{N}}\widetilde{D}^{\widetilde{S}}_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{N}},\\
&=(-1)^{\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{M}+\frac{3}{2}-\lambda_{N}}\widetilde{D}^{\widetilde{S}}_{\widetilde{M}\lambda_{N}}\ \text{(for $S_{R}=1$)},\end{split}

(11.32)

where
S_{R}
is the spin of the meson resonance that decays (a
\rho
meson, in this example). This property will be used later in this article.

Chapter 12
General Expressions for the Angular Distributions and Non-Recoil Polarization Observables

We will now derive the general expression for the angular distribution
W
when an arbitrary number of quasi-two-body states of arbitrary
spin contribute to the reaction, with interference effects included.

12.1
General Expression of the Decay Distribution in Terms of the SDME’s

As we have seen, the distribution will be a sum of different terms that contain
different submatrices,

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sum_{i}\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\hat{\rho}(S_{i})\hat{D}^{S_{i}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]+\sum_{i>j}\bigg\{\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{j}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}\quad+\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{j}\dagger}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\hat{\xi}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{D}^{S_{i}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})]\bigg\}.\end{split}

(12.1)

The indices
i
and
j
are used to label the spins of all of the contributing intermediate state hadrons contributing to the reaction.
The
\hat{\rho}(S_{i})
’s are the spin density matrices of the intermediate states. The
\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})
are the interference spin density matrices between the hadrons labeled by the indices
i
and
j
. Note that the sum goes over
i>j
terms so as to not overcount the interfering hadron pairs. The
\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{i}}
are the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices.

By using the hermiticity condition of the interference submatrices,

\hat{\xi}(S_{j},S_{i})=\hat{\xi}^{\dagger}(S_{i},S_{j}),

(12.2)

we can rewrite the second term inside of the brackets in the previous expression as

\displaystyle\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{j}\dagger}\hat{\xi}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{D}^{S_{i}}]=\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{j}\dagger}\hat{\xi}^{\dagger}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}],

(12.3)

\displaystyle=\Big(\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}}\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{j}\dagger}]\Big)^{*}.

Equation
12.1
can therefore be written as

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}\hat{\rho}(S_{i})\hat{D}^{S_{i}}]+\sum_{i>j}\bigg\{\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{j}}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\qquad+\Big(\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}}\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{j}}]\Big)^{*}\bigg\}

(12.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}\hat{\rho}(S_{i})\hat{D}^{S_{i}}]+\sum_{i>j}2\Re\Tr[\hat{D}^{S_{i}\dagger}\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{D}^{S_{j}}].

Written using index notation, the expression is

W=\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\eta}D^{S_{i}*}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\eta}2\Re\Big[D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\xi_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})D^{S_{j}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\Big].

(12.5)

Since the diagonal matrices are hermitian,
not all of its elements are independent. We can therefore simplify the expression by writing it only in terms of the independent elements,
which are the diagonal and top diagonal ones (
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
and
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda
, respectively). The first term in the last
equation can therefore be written as

\begin{split}\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\eta}D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}&=\sum_{\lambda,\eta}D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda,\eta}\Big\{D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}+D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\rho_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\Big\}.\end{split}

(12.6)

The first term contains the diagonal terms. Inside the brackets, the first term is a sum over top diagonal elements, and the second is a
sum over the bottom diagonal elements. Using the hermitian property of the diagonal matrix,

\begin{split}\hat{\rho}(S_{i})&=\hat{\rho}^{\dagger}(S_{i}),\\
\rho(S_{i})_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}&=\rho^{*}(S_{i})_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},\end{split}

(12.7)

the expression inside the brackets becomes

\begin{split}D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}+D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\rho_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}&=D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}+\Big(D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\Big)^{*}\\
&=2\Re[D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}].\end{split}

(12.8)

Substituting this last equation into eqn. (
12.5
), we get

\begin{split}W&=\sum_{i}\Bigg\{\sum_{\lambda,\eta}D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda>\lambda^{\prime}\\
\eta\end{subarray}}2\Re\Big[D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})D^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\Big]\Bigg\}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\eta}2\Re\Big[D^{*S_{i}}_{\lambda,\eta}\xi_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})D^{S_{j}}_{\lambda^{\prime},\eta}\Big].\end{split}

(12.9)

The first term in the brackets is a sum over diagonal elements, which are real.
Therefore, this expression makes explicit the fact that the distribution is a real quantity.

The
\phi^{*}
dependence of the distribution occurs only in the Wigner-
D
functions and in a very simple way,

D^{S}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=e^{-i\lambda\phi^{*}}d^{S}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*}),

(12.10)

where these Wigner
d
-functions are well known. Substituting eqn. (
12.10
) into (
12.9
) and using the following identity for
complex numbers,

\Re[z_{1}z_{2}]=\Re[z_{1}]\Re[z_{2}]-\Im[z_{1}]\Im[z_{2}],

(12.11)

we get,

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sum_{i}\Bigg\{\sum_{\lambda}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\lambda}(\theta^{*})\rho_{\lambda,\lambda}(S_{i})+\sum_{\lambda>\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Bigg(\Re[\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\Bigg)\Bigg\}\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Bigg(\Re[\xi_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Im[\xi_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\Bigg)\end{split}

(12.12)

where

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})&\equiv 2\sum_{\eta}d^{S_{i}}_{\eta,\lambda}(\theta^{*})d^{S_{i}}_{\eta,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*}),\\
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})&\equiv 2\sum_{\eta}d^{S_{i}}_{\eta,\lambda}(\theta^{*})d^{S_{j}}_{\eta,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*}).\end{split}

(12.13)

Therefore, the entire dependence on
\theta^{*}
is contained in the functions
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})
and

\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})
, which are simply bi-linear combinations of the well known
d
-functions, while
the entire
\phi^{*}
dependence is only contained in cosine and sine functions. Every term in the expression for
W
will either be
proportional to a cosine or sine function of
\phi^{*}
, or be independent of it.

Eqn. (
12.12
) is general for any photoproduction reaction with two mesons in the final state that has any number of
quasi-two-body intermediate states of arbitrary spin contributing to the process. If we apply it to the case where only one
intermediate state contributes (i.e., the index
i
runs over only one value and the term with the sum over both
i
and
j
vanishes) with a decaying vector meson V (
S_{i}=1
), we recover eqn. (
8.13
). If the intermediate states contains instead a decaying spin-3/2 baryon
B_{3/2}
(
S_{i}=3/2
), we recover eqn. (
8.20
).

12.2
General Expression of Observables in Terms of SDME’s

Since the decomposition of the spin density matrix into 16 matrices shown in eqn. (
6.15
) is general for any final state whose initial state has two particles each with two possible spin projections, and since it can be shown to also apply to interefence matrices, the matrices in eqn. (
12.12
) can therefore also be decomposed. This will give us an expression of the form shown in eqn. (
9.5
), with each
W^{i}
given by

\begin{split}W^{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sum_{k}\Bigg\{\sum_{\lambda}\widetilde{d}^{S_{k}}_{\lambda,\lambda}(\theta^{*})\rho^{i}_{\lambda,\lambda}(S_{k})+\sum_{\lambda>\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{k}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Bigg(\Re[\rho^{i}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Im[\rho^{i}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\Bigg)\Bigg\}\\
&\hphantom{=}\sum_{l>k}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{k},S_{l}}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Bigg(\Re[\xi^{i}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\\
&\hphantom{=}-\Im[\xi^{i}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*})]\Bigg),\end{split}

(12.14)

the matrix elements are given by

\begin{split}\rho^{0}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}},\\
\rho^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{N}\lambda_{H}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{\gamma}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}},\\
\rho^{i(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}},\\
\rho^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})&=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}\sigma^{j}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}},\end{split}

(12.15)

and

\displaystyle\xi^{0}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}},

\displaystyle\xi^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{N}\lambda_{H}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}},

(12.16)

\displaystyle\xi^{i(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}},

\displaystyle\xi^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda^{\prime}_{\gamma}\\
\lambda_{N}\lambda^{\prime}_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sigma^{i}_{\lambda_{\gamma}^{\prime}\lambda_{\gamma}}\sigma^{j}_{\lambda_{N}^{\prime}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}},

and
\lambda_{H}
is the helicity of the spectator hadron, the one that does not decay, either a meson or a baryon. In the case of the interference matrices
\hat{\xi}
,
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are the helicities of the two hadrons that are interfering, whose spins are
S_{k}
and
S_{l}
. We can use the expressions for the Pauli matrices in terms of the Kronecker delta in eqn. (
9
) to simplify the previous expressions. For simplicity, we will show the simplified expressions only for the interference matrices, with the understanding that the only difference between them and the non-interference matrices is that in the latter, the indices
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
refer both to helicities of a single hadron. The simplified expression for the density matrix related to the unpolarized cross section is

\xi^{0}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}.

(12.17)

The ones for the density matrices related to the beam observables are

\begin{split}&\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};-\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
&\xi^{y(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=-\frac{i}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\gamma}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};-\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\\
&\xi^{z(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\gamma}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}},\end{split}

(12.18)

and the ones related to the target observables are

\displaystyle\xi^{x(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}},

\displaystyle\xi^{y(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=-\frac{i}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}2\lambda_{N}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{N}\lambda_{\gamma}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N}},

(12.19)

\displaystyle\xi^{z(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k},S_{l})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}\\
\lambda_{H}\end{subarray}}2\lambda_{N}M_{\lambda\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}M^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{H};\lambda_{\gamma}\lambda_{N}}.

We will not show the expressions for the ones related to the beam/target double polarization observables, but it is easy to see from the pattern of the previous expressions what they would look like. The first pattern is that matrices with superscript of 2 and 3 will have an extra factor of
-\lambda_{\gamma}
or
-2\lambda_{N}
in the summation, the former for those with
(\gamma)
in the superscript and the latter for those with
(N)
in the superscript. Additionally, those with a superscript of 2 will have an extra factor of
i
. The second pattern is that matrices with superscript 1 and 2 will have the indices
\lambda_{\gamma}
or
\lambda_{N}
with opposite signs in each of the helicity amplitudes, the former for those with
(\gamma)
in the superscript and the latter for those with
(N)
in the superscript. These patterns will be important in deriving parity relations among these matrix elements. These in turn will be used to show which of the polarization observables will be even and which will be odd in
\phi^{*}
.

To relate the
W^{i}
’s in eqn. (
12.14
) to the polarization observables we follow the same procedure used in section
9
for the case of vector meson decays. To summarize, the trace is taken on both sides of eqn. (
7
). The terms on the right-hand side proportional to the Pauli matrices,
\hat{\sigma}^{i}
, vanish, while the left-hand side is equal to
W
. This expression is then compared to eqn. (
9.5
) to show that each
W^{i}
is proportional to a polarization observable. We reproduce the results here for convenience,

\begin{split}W^{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{i(N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}P^{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{x(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}\cos 2\beta I^{c}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{y(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}\sin 2\beta I^{s}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{z(\gamma)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}I^{\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{ix(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}\cos 2\beta P^{ic}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{iy(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}\sin 2\beta P^{is}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}),\\
W^{iz(\gamma N)}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=I_{0}P^{i\odot}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*}).\end{split}

(12.20)

12.3
General Parity Relations of the SDME’s

If the reaction is parity conserving, the helicity amplitudes can be related by equation (
10.4
). When this is applied to eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
), we get that the parity relations of the 16 matrices will have the form

\begin{split}\xi_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}&=\zeta(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},\\
\zeta&\in\{1,-1\}.\end{split}

(12.21)

The value of
\zeta
depends on three factors: its superscript (e.g.,
x(\gamma),z(N),zy(\gamma N)
, etc.), the intrinsic parities of the two unstable hadrons that are interfering, and on whether the density matrix describes the interference between mesons or baryons. We will now derive the previous equation and the conditions that determine the value of
\zeta
.

When the parity relations are applied to eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
), four factors will appear, each equal to either
1
or
-1
. Two of them are related to the properties of the hadrons involved, while the other two are related to the superscript of the 16 matrices.

The first factor has to do with the intrinsic parities of the hadrons involved in the reaction. When the parity relations are applied to eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
), this factor appears:

\xi_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto\eta\eta^{\prime}(\eta_{H})^{2}(\eta_{\gamma}\eta_{N})^{-2}\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=\eta\eta^{\prime}\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

(12.22)

where
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are the intrinsic parities of the two unstable resonances that are interfering, and
H
labels the spectator hadron. The factors that are raised to the power of two are equal to one because all
\eta=\pm 1
. For the non-interference matrices
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
, so that the factor
\eta\eta^{\prime}
in the previous equation will equal
1
. This last equation tells us that, when the parity relations of the helicity amplitudes are applied in eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
), the overall expression may gain a factor of
-1
depending on the intrinsic parities of the unstable hadrons: If they have opposite parities, the expression will gain a factor of
-1
.

The second factor depends on whether the spectator hadron is a meson or a baryon. This is because of the factor

\xi_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto(-1)^{(\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}-2\lambda_{H})}\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=\pm(-1)^{(\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})}\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}

(12.23)

where
\lambda_{H}
is the helicity of the spectator hadron. If this hadron is a baryon, the expression gets a minus sign because
2\lambda_{H}
is twice a half-integer, which is always an odd number. If it is a meson, the expression does not get the minus sign because
2\lambda_{H}
is twice an integer, which is always an even number. Since the unstable resonance will be a meson (baryon) when the spectator hadron is a baryon (meson), we can equivalently say that the density matrix describing mesons will get a factor of
-1
, while those describing baryons will not.

The third factor depends on which helicity indices on the right-hand side of eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
) have minus signs. For example, all of the spin density matrices that have superscripts of
0
or
z
will have the factor (the symbol “
\ |\
” represents the word “or”)

\xi^{0|z}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto(-1)^{2(\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N})}\xi^{0|z}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=-\xi^{0|z}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

(12.24)

those with superscripts of
x
or
y
with
(\gamma)
will have

\xi^{x|y(\gamma)}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto(-1)^{-2\lambda_{N}}\xi^{x|y(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=-\xi^{x|y(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

(12.25)

and those with superscripts of
x
or
y
with
(N)
will have

\xi^{x|y(N)}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto(-1)^{2\lambda_{\gamma}}\xi^{x|y(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=\xi^{x|y(N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}.

(12.26)

Eqn. (
12.24
) gets a
-1
because
2(\lambda_{\gamma}-\lambda_{N})
is twice the difference of a integer with a half-integer, which is always an odd integer. Eqn. (
12.25
) gets a
-1
because
-2\lambda_{N}
is twice a half-integer, which is always an odd integer. Eqn. (
12.26
) gets a
1
because
2\lambda_{\gamma}
is twice an integer, which is always an even integer. For the density matrices that have
(\gamma N)
in their superscript, both factors that appear in the associated matrices with superscript
(\gamma)
and
(N)
appear. For example,

\xi^{xz(\gamma N)}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}\propto-\xi^{xz(\gamma N)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}

(12.27)

because
\xi^{x(\gamma)}
would get a factor of
-1
while
\xi^{z(N)}
would get a factor of
1
.

The fourth factor is related to whether the expression on the right-hand side of eqns. (
12.17
) to (
12.2
) have a factor of
\lambda_{\gamma}
or
\lambda_{N}
in the summation. This follows from this identity, which is valid for any arbitrary function of an index
f(\lambda)
,

\sum_{\lambda}\lambda f(-\lambda)=-\sum_{\lambda}\lambda f(\lambda).

(12.28)

The transformation
\lambda\rightarrow-\lambda
can be done because
\lambda
is a dummy index. Therefore, all matrices with superscript
2
or
3
will get a factor of
-1
. If the matrix has two indices in its superscript, you multiply the factors that would have appeared for the two associated matrices that have those indices. For example, the matrix
\xi^{xz(\gamma N)}
would gets a factor of
-1
because
\xi^{x(\gamma)}
would get a factor of
1
while
\xi^{z(N)}
would get a factor of
-1
.

When all of the previous properties are taken into account, we find that the parity relations of the 16 matrices are

\displaystyle\xi^{0}_{-\lambda,-\lambda^{\prime}}=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}\zeta^{0}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\xi^{0}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\xi^{i(\gamma)}_{-\lambda,-\lambda^{\prime}}=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}\zeta^{(\gamma)i}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\xi^{i(\gamma)}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},

(12.29)

\displaystyle\xi^{i(N)}_{-\lambda,-\lambda^{\prime}}=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}\zeta^{(N)i}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\xi^{i(N)}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\xi^{ij(\gamma N)}_{-\lambda,-\lambda^{\prime}}=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}\zeta^{(\gamma)i}\zeta^{(N)j}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\xi^{ij(\gamma N)}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\bar{\eta}\equiv\eta\eta^{\prime},

where
\eta\eta^{\prime}
are the intrinsic parities of the two unstable hadrons that interfere. Therefore,
\bar{\eta}
will be called the intrinsic parity factor. For non-interference spin density matrices, this factor will always equals
1
.
S^{*}
is the spin of either one of the interfering hadrons. Since they will both have either integer or half-integer spin, it does not matter which one is used. Since the factor
(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}
depends on whether the unstable hadron is a fermion or a boson, we will call this the statistics factor. The remaining factor depends only on the type of matrix, and are given by

\begin{split}\zeta^{0}&=-1,\\
\zeta^{i(\gamma)}&=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1,&\text{if }i=y,z\\
-1,&\text{if }i=x\end{cases},\\
\zeta^{i(N)}&=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1,&\text{if }i=x,z\\
-1,&\text{if }i=y.\end{cases}.\end{split}

(12.30)

We will therefore call this the SDME factor. As an example, suppose we want to find the parity relations for the non-interference spin density matrix
\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S=2)
, for the decay of a spin-2 pseudoscalar meson. In this case,
\bar{\eta}=1
because it is a non-interference matrix,
(-1)^{2S^{*}+1}=-1
because
S^{*}=2
, and
\zeta^{z(\gamma)}=1
from eqn. (
12.30
). The parity relation will therefore be

\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(S=2)=-(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\rho^{z(\gamma)}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(S=2).

(12.31)

12.4
Parity Considerations in General Observables in Terms of the SDME’s

We will now show why when the reaction is parity conserving only terms that are proportional to either cosine or sine functions will
appear in the expression. From the definitions of the
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}
and
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}
in eqn. (
12.13
) it can be shown that

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}&=\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\\
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}&=\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}.\end{split}

(12.32)

Also, the property

d^{S_{i}}_{-\eta-\lambda}=(-1)^{\eta-\lambda}d^{S_{i}}_{\eta\lambda},

(12.33)

can be used to show that,

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{2S_{i}}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}},\\
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{2S_{i}}(-1)^{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}.\end{split}

(12.34)

These last equations along with eqns. (
12.3
), the hermiticity condition in
eqn. (
4.8
), the even and odd property of the
trigonometric functions, and the following properties from the real and imaginary part functions,

\begin{split}\Re[z^{*}]&=\Re[z],\\
\Im[z^{*}]&=-\Im[z],\\
z&\in\mathbb{C},\end{split}

(12.35)

can be used to shown that

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}^{S_{k}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Re[\rho^{i}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}]&=\zeta^{i}\widetilde{d}^{S_{k}}_{-\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}(\theta^{*})\Re[\rho^{i}_{-\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}(S_{i})]\cos[(-\lambda^{\prime}+\lambda)\phi^{*}],\\
\widetilde{d}^{S_{k}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Im[\rho^{i}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{k})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}]&=-\zeta^{i}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i}}_{-\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}(\theta^{*})\Im[\rho_{-\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}(S_{k})]\sin[(-\lambda^{\prime}+\lambda)\phi^{*}].\end{split}

(12.36)

Since
\zeta^{i}=\pm 1
, this means that applying the parity relations to each
W^{i}
will give you either a plus or minus sign to each of the terms proportional to the real part function and the opposite sign to each of the terms proportional to the imaginary part function. The terms that get the minus signs will always cancel with another term, and therefore either all terms will be proportional to the cosine function or all terms will be proportional to the sine function will vanish. For example, the expression for
W^{x(\gamma)}
for a reaction with a vector meson
V
intermediate state with corresponding spin density matrix
\hat{\rho}^{x(\gamma)}(V)
will contain the following terms,

\begin{split}W^{x(\gamma)}&=\cdots+\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Re\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]+\widetilde{d}^{1}_{0-1}\Re\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{0-1}(V)\Big]\bigg)\cos(\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Im\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]+\widetilde{d}^{1}_{0-1}\Im\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{0-1}(V)\Big]\bigg)\sin(\phi^{*})+\cdots,\\
&=\cdots+\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Re\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]+\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Re\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]\bigg)\cos(\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Im\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]-\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Im\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]\bigg)\sin(\phi^{*})+\cdots,\\
&=\cdots+2\widetilde{d}^{1}_{10}\Re\Big[\rho^{x(\gamma)}_{10}(V)\Big]\cos(\phi^{*})+\cdots.\end{split}

(12.37)

For the terms that contain the interference matrices, the property that the cosine function is even while the sine function is odd can be used to show this identity,

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Re[\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\cos[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}]&=\pm\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Re[\xi_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\cos[(-\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}],\\
\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Im[\xi_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\sin[(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}]&=\mp\widetilde{d}^{S_{i},S_{j}}_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta^{*})\Im[\xi_{-\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})]\sin[(-\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})\phi^{*}],\end{split}

(12.38)

which imply that for the terms proportional to the interference matrices, once again either all the terms proportional to the cosine
functions vanish or all the terms proportional to the sine functions vanish. For example, the expression for
W^{x(\gamma)}
for a
reaction with a vector meson
V
resonance interfering with a spin-2 meson
T
resonance with corresponding spin density
matrix
\hat{\xi}^{x(\gamma)}(V,T)
will have the following terms,

\begin{split}W^{x(\gamma)}&=\cdots+\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Re\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]+\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{-12}\Re\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{-12}(V,T)\Big]\bigg)\cos(3\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Im\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]-\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{-12}\Im\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{-12}(V,T)\Big]\bigg)\sin(3\phi^{*})+\cdots,\\
&=\cdots+\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Re\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]+\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Re\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]\bigg)\cos(3\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}-\bigg(\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Im\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]-\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Im\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(V,T)\Big]\bigg)\sin(3\phi^{*})+\cdots,\\
&=\cdots+2\widetilde{d}^{1,2}_{1-2}\Re\Big[\xi^{x(\gamma)}_{1-2}(\rho)\Big]\cos(3\phi^{*})+\cdots.\end{split}

(12.39)

12.5
General Expression of the Decay Distribution in Terms of Statistical Tensors

We can also write the most general expression for the distribution, eqn. (
12.12
), in terms of the statistical tensors.
First, we need to note that just like a spin density matrix can be decomposed into a sum of polarization operators as shown in
eqn. (
5.5
), the same can be done for the interference spin density matrix between two hadrons of spins
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
,

\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})=\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{L=S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i}).

(12.40)

The matrix elements of the interference polarization tensors,
\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})
, are now defined as

\Big[T_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]_{m,m^{\prime}}\equiv(-1)^{S_{j}-m^{\prime}}C^{LM}_{S_{i},m;S_{j},-m^{\prime}},

(12.41)

and the
\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})
’s are the interference statistical tensors between two particles of spin
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
,

\begin{split}\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})&=\Big<\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\Big>=\Tr[\hat{\xi}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})]\\
&=\sum_{m=-S_{i}}^{S_{i}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-S_{j}}^{S_{j}}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}\xi_{m,m^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})C^{LM}_{S_{j},m^{\prime};S_{i},-m}.\end{split}

(12.42)

The inverse relation of the previous equation is

\xi_{mm^{\prime}}(S_{i},\ S_{j})=\sum_{L=-|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{M=L}\tau_{LM}(S_{i},\ S_{j})(-1)^{S_{j}-m}C^{LM}_{S_{j}m^{\prime};S_{i}-m}.

(12.43)

Substituting the expressions for spin density matrices in terms of statistical tensors, eqns. (
5.5
) and
(
12.40
), into equation (
12.1
), we get

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}t_{LM}(S_{i})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{i}}]\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\Big\{\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{j}}]\\
&\hphantom{=}+\tau_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{j}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{i}}]\Big\}.\end{split}

(12.44)

This expression can be further simplified by using certain properties of the polarization operators,
\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{i})

and
\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})
, and of the statistical tensors,
t_{LM}(S_{i})
and
\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})
. By using these properties
of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients,

\begin{split}C^{LM}_{Sm;S^{\prime}m^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{S+S^{\prime}-L}C^{LM}_{S^{\prime}m^{\prime};Sm},\\
C^{LM}_{Sm;S^{\prime}m^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{S+S^{\prime}-L}C^{L-M}_{S-m;S^{\prime}-m^{\prime}},\end{split}

(12.45)

these properties of the elements of the polarization operators can be shown,

\begin{split}[T_{LM}(S,S^{\prime})]_{mm^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{M+S-S^{\prime}}[T_{L-M}(S^{\prime},S)]_{m^{\prime}m},\\
[T_{LM}(S,S^{\prime})]_{mm^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{L+S-S^{\prime}}[T_{L-M}(S,S^{\prime})]_{-m-m^{\prime}},\\
[T_{LM}(S,S^{\prime})]_{mm^{\prime}}&=(-1)^{L+M}[T_{LM}(S^{\prime},S)]_{-m^{\prime}-m}.\end{split}

(12.46)

They can also be used to show a generalized hermiticity property of the interference statistical tensors,

\tau_{LM}(S,S^{\prime})=(-1)^{S-S^{\prime}+M}\tau^{*}_{L-M}(S^{\prime},S).

(12.47)

We can use these last two equations to rewrite the second term in the brackets in eqn.
(
12.44
),

\begin{split}&\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\tau_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{j}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{i}}]\\
&\qquad\qquad=\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\Big(\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{j}}]\Big)^{*},\end{split}

(12.48)

where we have used the fact that, since the index
M
is a dummy index, when all of them in the expression are replaced by
-M

the summation remains the same. Eqn. (
12.44
) therefore becomes

\begin{split}W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}t_{LM}(S_{i})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{i}}]\\
&\hphantom{=}+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}2\Re\Big\{\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{j}}]\Big\}.\end{split}

(12.49)

We will now show how the two traces found in the previous equation can be further simplified. We will show it for the trace in the second
term, since the same derivation applies to the one in the first term but with
S_{i}=S_{j}
. In index notation, and for recoil baryon
B

having spin
s
, that trace is

\Tr[\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger S_{i}}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{S_{j}}]=\sum_{m=-S_{i}}^{S_{i}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-S_{j}}^{S_{j}}\sum_{n=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}\mathcal{D}^{*S_{i}}_{m,n}C^{LM}_{S_{j},m^{\prime};S_{i},-m}\mathcal{D}^{S_{j}}_{m^{\prime},n}.

(12.50)

Certain properties of the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions and the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients will be used. First,

\mathcal{D}^{S}_{m,m^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m^{\prime}-m}\mathcal{D}^{*S}_{-m,-m^{\prime}}.

(12.51)

Second is the Clebsch-Gordan series for the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions,

\mathcal{D}^{S_{1}}_{m_{1},n_{1}}\mathcal{D}^{S_{2}}_{m_{2},n_{2}}=\sum_{J=|S_{1}-S_{2}|}^{J=S_{1}+S_{2}}\sum_{M,N=-J}^{J}C^{JM}_{S_{1},m_{1};S_{2},m_{2}}\mathcal{D}^{J}_{M,N}C^{JN}_{S_{1},n_{1};S_{2},n_{2}}.

(12.52)

Third is the orthogonality condition of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients,

\sum_{m_{1}=-j_{1}}^{j_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}=-j_{2}}^{j_{2}}C^{JM}_{j_{1},m_{1};j_{2},m_{2}}C^{J^{\prime}M^{\prime}}_{j_{1},m_{1};j_{2},m_{2}}=\delta_{JJ^{\prime}}\delta_{MM^{\prime}}.

(12.53)

Finally, a relation between the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions and the spherical harmonics,

\mathcal{D}^{L}_{M,0}(\theta,\phi)=\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2L+1}}(-1)^{M}Y_{L,-M}(\theta,\phi).

(12.54)

Using these last four equations, we can rewrite eqn. (
12.49
) in the simplified form

\begin{split}&W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};s)=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}2\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)\Re\Big\{(-1)^{M}\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big\},\end{split}

(12.55)

where

\begin{split}\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)&\equiv\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2L+1}}\Tr[\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{L0}(S_{i})]\\
&=\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2L+1}}\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{L0}_{S_{i},m;S_{i},-m},\\
\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)&\equiv\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2L+1}}\Tr[\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{L0}(S_{j},\ S_{i})]\\
&=\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2L+1}}\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{L0}_{S_{j},m;S_{i},-m}.\end{split}

(12.56)

This last equation shows that, when expressing the distribution
W
in terms of the statistical tensors, it acquires a very simple form.
First, every single contributing hadron of spin
S_{i}
will contribute a sum over the well known spherical harmonics for

0\leq L\leq 2S_{i}
, multiplied by the statistical tensors of the corresponding values for
L
and
M
. The entire angular dependence
for the decay of the hadron,
\theta^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
, is contained in them. The prefactor for each term,
\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)
,
depends only on the spin of the decaying hadron
S_{i}
, and the spin of the daughter hadron
s
. As seen in their definition in eqn.
(
12.56
), they are easily calculated for any combination of
S_{i}
and
s
, since they are a linear combination of the well
known Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. Second, every pair of contributing hadrons with spins
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
will also contribute a linear
combination of spherical harmonics, this time for
|S_{i}-S_{j}|\leq L\leq S_{i}+S_{j}
. The prefactor,
\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)
,
is now a function of the spins of the interfering hadrons and the spin of the daughter hadron, but has a similar simple definition form as
the previous one.

Note that this expression makes explicit the fact that distribution is a rotational scalar, since the contractions

\sum_{M}(-1)^{M}t_{LM}Y_{L-M}
and
\sum_{M}(-1)^{M}\tau_{LM}Y_{L-M}
are rotational invariants,

\displaystyle\sum_{M}(-1)^{M}t_{LM}Y_{L-M}\ \longrightarrow

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\sum_{M,M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}}(-1)^{M}D^{L}_{MM^{\prime}}D^{L}_{-M-M^{\prime\prime}}t_{LM^{\prime}}Y_{L-M^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{M,M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}}(-1)^{M+M^{\prime\prime}-M}D^{L}_{MM^{\prime}}D^{L*}_{MM^{\prime\prime}}t_{LM^{\prime}}Y_{L-M^{\prime\prime}}

(12.57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}}(-1)^{M^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{M^{\prime}M^{\prime\prime}}t_{LM^{\prime}}Y_{L-M^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{M}(-1)^{M}t_{LM}Y_{L-M}\ }\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\\
\hphantom{M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{M}t_{LM}Y_{L-M}.

One property should be noted about the coefficients
\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)
. By using the properties of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients in
eqn. (
12.45
), and using the fact that
(-1)^{2(S_{i}-m)=1}
for all
S_{i}
and
m
(since
S_{i}
and
m
are
both integer or half-integer,
2(S_{i}-m)
is twice an integer number, which is always an even number), we can rewrite

\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)
as

\begin{split}&\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{L0}_{S_{i},m;S_{i},-m}=\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m+m-m+2S_{i}-L}C^{L0}_{S_{i},-m;S_{i},m}\\
&\qquad=\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}+m}(-1)^{2(S_{i}-m)}(-1)^{-L}C^{L0}_{S_{i},-m;S_{i},m}\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{L}\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}+m}C^{L0}_{S_{i},-m;S_{i},m}.\end{split}

(12.58)

In the last line, we used the fact that
(-1)^{-L}=(-1)^{L}
since
L
is an integer. Using the last equation, we can see that

\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)
has the property

\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)=(-1)^{L}\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)\Rightarrow\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)=0\qquad\text{for}\ L\ \text{odd}.

(12.59)

Therefore, in the first term in eqn. (
12.55
), only the terms with even
L
appear. Applying the same procedure to
\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)
, it can be shown that for the interference tensors
\tau_{LM}
, the
L
odd terms vanish only if
S_{i}=S_{j}
.

This shows that not all tensors will appear on the expressions for the angular distribution and polarization observables. This may seem confusing at first, because this would seem to imply that the general expressions for the decay distribution in terms of the SDME’s has more independent parameters than those in terms of the statistical tensors. However, upon closer inspection, we notice that when expressed in terms of the SDME’s, the coefficients of each of the trigonometric functions in the sum are actually
specific
linear combination of the SDME’s. For example, in the case of vector meson decay, its density matrix has a total of 9 parameters. When expressed in terms of the statistical tensors in eqn. (
8.23
), only 6 independent parameters appear. But note that when expressed in terms of the SDME’s as shown in eqn. (
2.1
), there are only 6 coefficients because 3 of them are linear combinations of two SDME’s. This is another reason that shows how using statistical tensors to represent the spin state of a mixed quantum state is more natural than with spin density matrices.

The last step needed to simplify the general expression in eqn. (
12.55
) is to use the hermiticity condition of the non-interference tensors shown in eqn. (
5.10
), that shows that these tensors are not all independent. We first split the sum of the non-interference tensors, and then apply the hermiticity condition, and the Spherical Harmonic identity,

Y_{LM}=(-1)^{M}Y_{L-M}^{*},

(12.60)

and the identity for complex numbers shown in eqn. (
12.11
) to get

\begin{split}&\sum_{M=-L}^{L}(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})=t_{L0}(S_{i})Y_{L,0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\sum_{M>0}^{L}\bigg\{(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+(-1)^{-M}t_{L-M}(S_{i})Y_{L,M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\bigg\}\\
&\qquad=t_{L0}(S_{i})Y_{L,0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+\sum_{M>0}^{L}\bigg\{(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\Big[(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]^{*}\bigg\}\\
&\qquad=t_{L0}(S_{i})Y_{L,0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+2\sum_{M>0}^{L}\Re\Big[(-1)^{M}t_{LM}(S_{i})Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\\
&\qquad=t_{L0}(S_{i})Y_{L,0}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})+2\sum_{M>0}^{L}(-1)^{M}\bigg(\Re\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\Re\Big[Y_{L-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\\
&\qquad\quad-\Im\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\Im\Big[Y_{L-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\bigg).\end{split}

(12.61)

Note that the hermiticity condition of the tensor implies that
t_{L0}
is purely real. The previous expression is therefore manifestly purely real, as expected. The general expression therefore becomes

\begin{split}&W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};s)=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M\geq 0}^{L}2\kappa^{L}(S_{i};s)(-1)^{M}\bigg(\Re\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\Re\Big[Y_{L-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\\
&\qquad\quad-\Im\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\Im\Big[Y_{L-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\bigg)\\
&\qquad+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}2\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)(-1)^{M}\bigg(\Re\Big[\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\Re\Big[Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\\
&\qquad\quad-\Im\Big[\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\Im\Big[Y_{L,-M}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})\Big]\bigg),\end{split}

(12.62)

where we have also used the complex numbers identity in eqn. (
12.11
) to rewrite the expression in parenthesis in the second term in terms of real and imaginary parts of
\tau
. To simplify the notation, we included
t_{L0}
inside the real and imaginary parts functions so that the sum can go over
M\geq 0
, but it must be remembered that
\Im[t_{L0}]=0
. Note that in the second term of the previous equation the hermiticity relation for the interference tensor in eqn. (
12.47
) cannot be used to reduce the number of terms in the sum, because it relates
\tau(S_{i},S_{j})
and
\tau(S_{j},S_{i})
, which are different tensors. Therefore, for the interference tensors the summation over
M
goes from
-L
to
L
.
If we want the expression in terms of sine and cosine functions of
\phi^{*}
, we can write the spherical harmonics in terms of the associated Legendre polynomials,

\begin{split}Y_{LM}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*})&=\sqrt{\frac{(2L+1)(L-M)!}{4\pi(L+M)!}}P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})e^{iM\phi^{*}}\\
&=\sqrt{\frac{(2L+1)(L-M)!}{4\pi(L+M)!}}P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})(\cos(M\phi^{*})+i\sin(M\phi^{*})).\end{split}

(12.63)

Note how the entire
\phi^{*}
dependence is contained in the cosine and sine function. With this, we get for the general expression,

\begin{split}&W(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};s)=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M\geq 0}^{L}\widetilde{\kappa}^{LM}(S_{i};s)P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\Im\Big[t_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\bigg)\\
&\qquad+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}\widetilde{\kappa}^{LM}(S_{i},S_{j};s)P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re\Big[\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\Im\Big[\tau_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\bigg),\end{split}

(12.64)

where

\begin{split}\widetilde{\kappa}^{LM}(S_{i};s)&\equiv 2\sqrt{\frac{(L-M)!}{(L+M)!}}\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{L0}_{S_{j}m;S_{i}-m},\\
\widetilde{\kappa}^{LM}(S_{i},S_{j};s)&\equiv 2\sqrt{\frac{(L-M)!}{(L+M)!}}\sum_{m=-s}^{s}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{L0}_{S_{j}m;S_{i}-m},\end{split}

(12.65)

and we eliminated the factor of
(-1)^{M}
by using the identity

P^{-M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})=(-1)^{M}P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*}).

(12.66)

12.6
General Expressions of Observables in Terms of Statistical Tensors

The general expression for the 16
W^{i}
’s shown in eqn. (
12.14
) can also be expressed in terms of the statistical tensors. The decomposition of the statistical tensors
t
in terms or 16 other tensors shown in eqn. (
7.42
) also apply to the interference tensors
\tau
. The relationship between the 16 tensors to the 16 matrices has the same form as eqn. (
12.43
), but with a superscript to label which member of the 16 matrices or tensors it refers to,

\begin{split}\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})&=\Big<\hat{\xi^{i}}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})\Big>=\Tr[\hat{\xi^{i}}(S_{i},S_{j})\hat{T}_{LM}(S_{j},S_{i})]\\
&=\sum_{m=-S_{i}}^{S_{i}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-S_{j}}^{S_{j}}(-1)^{S_{i}-m}\xi^{i}_{m,m^{\prime}}(S_{i},S_{j})C^{LM}_{S_{j},m^{\prime};S_{i},-m}.\end{split}

(12.67)

The inverse relation is

\begin{split}\xi^{i}_{mm^{\prime}}(S_{i},\ S_{j})&=\sum_{L=-|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{M=L}\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},\ S_{j})(-1)^{S_{i}-m}C^{LM}_{S_{j}m^{\prime};S_{i}-m}.\end{split}

(12.68)

By substituting this last expression into equation eqn. (
12.14
) we get the most general expression for the
W^{i}
’s in terms or the statistical tensors, which has the same form as the most general expression of the decay angular distribution in eqn. (
12.64
) but with the appropriate superscript,

\begin{split}&W^{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};s)=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M\geq 0}^{L}2\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i};s)P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re\Big[t^{i}_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\Im\Big[t^{i}_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\bigg)\\
&\qquad+\sum_{i>j}\sum_{L=|S_{i}-S_{j}|}^{S_{i}+S_{j}}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}2\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\bigg(\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\\
&\qquad\quad+\Im\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\bigg).\end{split}

(12.69)

Remember that, as shown in eqn. (
12.20
), the
W^{i}
are proportional to polarization observables.

12.7
General Parity Relations of the Statistical Tensors

To derive the parity relations of the statistical tensors, we need to use the parity relations of the SDME’s shown in eqn. (
12.3
), the relationship between the statistical tensors and the SDME’s shown in eqn. (
12.67
), and the properties of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients shown in eqn. (
12.45
). With these, the parity properties of the statistical tensors can be shown to be

\begin{split}t^{k}_{LM}(S_{i})&=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S_{i}+1}\zeta^{k}(-1)^{L+M+2S_{i}}t^{k}_{L-M}(S_{i}),\\
\tau^{k}_{LM}(S_{i},\ S_{j})&=\bar{\eta}(-1)^{2S_{i}+1}\zeta^{k}(-1)^{L+M+S_{i}+S_{j}}\tau^{k}_{L-M}(S_{i},\ S_{j}).\end{split}

(12.70)

This relations can be simplified by using the following properties,

\begin{split}&(-1)^{2S_{i}}(-1)^{2S_{i}}=(-1)^{4S_{i}}=1,\\
&(-1)^{2S_{i}}(-1)^{S_{i}+S_{j}}=(-1)^{2S_{i}+1}(-1)^{-S_{i}-S_{j}}=(-1)^{S_{i}-S_{j}}.\end{split}

(12.71)

These properties follow because
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
are either both integer or both half-integer. Therefore,
4S_{i}
is an even integer,
S_{i}+S_{j}
is an integer, and
(-1)^{n}=(-1)^{-n}
for any integer
n
. The parity relations of the statistical tensors are

\begin{split}t^{k}_{LM}(S_{i})&=-\bar{\eta}\zeta^{k}(-1)^{L+M}t^{k}_{L-M}(S_{i}),\\
\tau^{k}_{LM}(S_{i},\ S_{j})&=-\bar{\eta}\zeta^{k}(-1)^{L+M+S_{i}-S_{j}}\tau^{k}_{L-M}(S_{i},\ S_{j}),\end{split}

(12.72)

where the expressions for the
\zeta^{k}
were given in eqn. (
12.30
), and
\bar{\eta}
is the multiplication of the two intrinsic parities of the interfering hadrons.

12.8
Parity Considerations in General Observables in Terms of Statistical Tensors

Just as in the case of the SDME’s, when the scattering reaction is parity conserving, the expressions for the observables can be further simplified. Specifically, each of the
W^{i}
’s shown in eqn. (
12.69
) will be either an even or odd function of the variable
\phi^{*}
(i.e., all the terms proportional to either
\cos(M\phi^{*})
or
\sin(M\phi^{*})
vanish).

In the case of the non-interference tensors, parity conservation in the scattering process implies that each of the 16 tensors will be either purely real or purely imaginary. All that needs to be done to show this is use the hermiticity condition shown in eqn. (
5.10
) along with the parity relation shown in eqn. (
12.72
),

\begin{split}t^{k}_{LM}(S_{i})&=(-1)^{M}t^{k*}_{L-M}(S_{i})=-1(-1)^{L}\zeta^{k}t^{k*}_{LM}(S_{i}),\\
t^{k}_{LM}(S_{i})&=-\zeta^{k}t^{k*}_{LM}(S_{i})\end{split}

(12.73)

The factor of
(-1)^{L}
vanishes because only even terms appear in the sum for the non-interference tensors, as shown in eqn. (
12.59
). For the tensors of the type
t^{kl(\gamma N)}
, we instead have

t^{kl(\gamma N)}_{LM}(S_{i})=-\zeta^{k}\zeta^{kl}t^{k(\gamma N)*}_{LM}(S_{i}).

(12.74)

Since
\zeta^{k}=\pm 1
, the right-hand side of the second line of eqn. (
12.73
) will have a factor of either
1
or
-1
. This factor depends only on the value of the superscript
k
, not on the spin or the statistics of the decaying hadron. From the general property of complex numbers shown in eqn. (
12.35
), if this overall coefficient equals
1
then
t^{k}(S_{i})
is purely real, and if the coefficient is negative then it will be purely imaginary. The values of
\zeta^{k}
shown in eqn. (
12.30
) can be used to conclude that

\begin{split}\Re[t^{k}]&=0,\text{ if }\zeta^{k}=1\rightarrow\text{ Observable odd in }\phi^{*},\\
\Im[t^{k}]&=0,\text{ if }\zeta^{k}=-1\rightarrow\text{ Observable even in }\phi^{*},\\
&\\
\Re[t^{kl}]&=0,\text{ if }\zeta^{k}\zeta^{l}=1\rightarrow\text{ Observable odd in }\phi^{*},\\
\Im[t^{kl}]&=0,\text{ if }\zeta^{k}\zeta^{l}=-1\rightarrow\text{ Observable even in }\phi^{*}.\end{split}

(12.75)

Since the cosine functions of
\phi^{*}
are multiplied by real parts of tensors, while the sine functions are multiplied by imaginary parts, we conclude that the observable associated with
t^{k}
or
t^{kl}
will be even (odd) if
\zeta^{k}
or
\zeta^{kl}
equals
-1
(
1
). Table
12.1
summarizes the symmetry properties under
\phi^{*}\rightarrow-\phi^{*}
for the unpolarized and single polarization observables.

Table 12.1:
Symmetry of observables under
\phi^{*}\rightarrow-\phi^{*}
.

Symmetry of observables under
\phi^{*}\rightarrow-\phi^{*}

Even

Odd

I^{0}

I^{0}I^{s}

I^{0}I^{c}

I^{0}I^{\odot}

I^{0}P_{y}

I^{0}P_{x}

I^{0}P_{y}^{c}

I^{0}P_{z}

I^{0}P_{x}^{s}

I^{0}P_{x}^{c}

I^{0}P_{z}^{s}

I^{0}P_{z}^{c}

I^{0}P_{x}^{\odot}

I^{0}P_{y}^{s}

I^{0}P_{z}^{\odot}

I^{0}P_{y}^{\odot}

We can derive similar results for the interference tensors
\tau
, but using a different procedure. To do so, we need to make use of some properties of
\kappa_{L}
,
P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})
,
\cos(M\phi^{*})
,
\sin(M\phi^{*})
, and the parity relations for the interference tensors shown in the second line of eqn. (
12.72
). First, in the general expression for the
W^{i}
’s shown in eqn. (
12.69
), the summation over
M
in the second term is split into two parts: a sum over the
M>0
terms, and a sum over the
M<0
terms,

\displaystyle W^{i}(\theta^{*},\ \phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\cdots+\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)P^{0}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{L0}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\quad+\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)\sum_{M>0}^{L}\bigg(P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})

(12.76)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\quad+P^{-M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{L-M}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(-M\phi^{*})\bigg)+\cdots.

Four properties need to be used to simplify this last equation. First, using the properties of the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients shown in equation (
12.45
), it can be shown that

\kappa_{L}(S_{i},\ S_{j})=(-1)^{L+S_{i}-S_{j}}\kappa_{L}(S_{i},\ S_{j}).

(12.77)

Second, the property of the associated Legendre polynomials shown in eqn. (
12.66
). Third,

\cos(-M\phi^{*})=\cos(M\phi^{*}).

(12.78)

And fourth, the parity property shown in the second line of eqn. (
12.72
). Combining all these properties, eqn. (
12.8
) becomes

\displaystyle W^{i}(\theta^{*},\ \phi^{*})

\displaystyle=\cdots+\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)P^{0}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{L0}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\quad+\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)\sum_{M>0}^{L}\bigg(P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})

(12.79)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}\quad-\eta\zeta^{i}P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\bigg)+\cdots

Note the appearance of the factor
-\bar{\eta}\zeta^{i}
in the second term inside of the parenthesis. This same procedure can be used with the terms proportional to the imaginary parts of the tensors, but since

\sin(-M\phi^{*})=-\sin(-M\phi^{*}),

(12.80)

we get instead

\begin{split}W^{i}(\theta^{*},\ \phi^{*})&=\cdots+\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j};s)\sum_{M>0}^{L}\bigg(P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})(\theta^{*})\Im\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\\
&\hphantom{=}\quad+\eta\zeta^{i}P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Im\Big[\tau^{i}_{LM}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]\sin(M\phi^{*})\bigg)+\cdots.\end{split}

(12.81)

The term with
\Im[\tau_{L0}]
vanishes because
\sin(0)=0
. Note how we get an extra minus sign, so that now the factor is
\eta\zeta^{i}
. The only possible values of this factor are
1
and
-1
. If it is equal to
1
, the expression inside of the parenthesis in eqn. (
12.8
) vanishes and the observable will contain no terms proportional to cosines of
\phi^{*}
. If the factor instead equals
-1
, the expression inside of the parenthesis in eqn. (
12.81
) vanishes and the observable will contain no terms proportional to sines of
\phi^{*}
. This shows that once parity conservation is taken into account, the observables will be either even or odd in
\phi^{*}
. Also, for the term with
\Re[\tau^{i}_{L0}(S_{i},\ S_{j})]
, after applying all identities,

\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i},S_{j})P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*}))\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{L0}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big]=-\widetilde{\eta}\zeta^{i}\kappa^{L}(S_{i},S_{j})P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[\tau^{i}_{L0}(S_{i},S_{j})\Big].

(12.82)

From this we conclude that when the factor
\eta\zeta^{i}=1
, this term vanishes (it does not necessarily imply that
\Re[\tau_{L0}]=0
). As we’ve mentioned, when
\eta\zeta^{i}=1
, all terms proportional to
\cos(M\phi^{*})
vanish. We therefore conclude that the term with
\Re[\tau_{L0}]
only appears when the observable is even in
\phi^{*}
.

The value of the factor
\eta\zeta^{i}
only depends on two things. One is the intrinsic parity of the interacting hadrons. If their intrinsic parities are not the same,
\eta=-1
. It otherwise equals
1
. The other is which of the 16 tensors is related to the observables, since it determines the value of
\zeta^{i}
, which are shown in eqn. (
12.30
).

To summarize, once parity is taken into account, the expressions for the observables will either be

\begin{split}&W^{i}(\theta^{*},\phi^{*};s)=\sum_{i}\sum_{L=0}^{2S_{i}}\sum_{M\geq 0}^{L}2\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}(S_{i};s)P^{M}_{L}(\cos\theta^{*})\Re\Big[t^{i}_{LM}(S_{i})\Big]\cos(M\phi^{*})\\
